LADIES' LETTER.

MY DZAR Nmr...ux,

I have seen a very pretty blouse which is corm posed of a very soft shade of lilac silk, with the brightening addition of a fine white mousseline vest, and a bar of white braid-lace threaded through with lilac ribbon. There is one shade of lilac that suggests serenity and old-world peace more than any other colour, and it is the one that is allied so well with pure white. None of the red shades of purple, such as plum, that achieve ultimately the white tints so popular, Itre per- missible for half-mourning.

Millinery more _ Indefinite, but tl aspect of it as a whole is still low and broad, and much tulle is used, while wreaths of white leafless blossoms are held in high regard. In England so few frosted-leaf sprays have been seen that they should be tried upon half-mourning toques, and there are purple looking one, touched with frost, that really would be most appropriate. Does anyone miss the gourah plume that used to wave over every second smart toque seen? It seems to be taking a rest, and even ostrich feathers are less often seen than they were, owing to the liking for flower and leaf adornments, and the many fancy quills.

A very nice black-cloth gown is trimmed with "black silk strappings, five of them being placed at equal distances round the lower part. These strappings could be in cloth if so preferred. Cloth wears bettoi than silk. The bodice is chiefly made of cloth, but the front is almost covered with silk tucks, arranged in groups of five, each group out- lined with silk stitching. The right front crosses 11ight17 over, fastening by means of loops and little let buttons. The collar is in the wide, fiat shape that provides epaulettes. The upper part of it and the muff are made of astrachan. The hat was a stitched silk crown, and was trimmed with black silk poppies and glac6 silk, fastened on with jet buckles. This dress is very useful, as it is both smart and comfortable, without being in the least elaborate or expensive. The blcuse especially is at once warm and simple. The sleeves are in the new shape which widens at the wrists °vur pointed undersleeves.

The little silk coatees and the more expensive velvet ones will be as useful as any small wrap for March. They mark the transition from deep to hall-mourning, for they are most charmingly made of grey and purple shades in every variety, and for very smart wear even in white. London is too smoke-begrimed a city to permit white to be wear- able in winter, but fancy the charms of a white silk coatee, tucked all over as such little coats must be, or one of white velvet with delicate traceries of silver embroidery upon the edge. The salient feature of the velvet coatee should be a very narrow bordering of embroidery done in floss silks and silver. There are infinite varieties of purples and greys to be bought. Inquiry should be made for smoke, pavement, pastel, and silver grey, and for amethyst, hyacinth, orchid, and eminence purple. The most fashionable ruffles are of accordioned glaeiS silk edged with the narrowest possible hand e! fur, or with velvet-ribbon. Ostrich-feather boas —which seem likely to retain their popularity in- definitely—are very short and full and their ends are finished with falls of lace; black, white, or " old-lace " colour according to taste.

A dress a friend was wearing was composed of black Irish frieze strapped with Melton cloth or black silk, the strappings being outlined with silver. The lines of stra.ppings round the skirt are inclulating waves. The vest, collar and cuffs are in white panne, trimmed with stripes of black cloth and furni.lie,l wiLh tautens and loops a black cord. The undersleeves are white panne. The short-basqued coat is of a shape that promises to be much worn when the weather is milder, and the collar may also be taken as a model of the mode to come.

The straight-fronted corset is universally worn smart women, and is undeniably a most rational style. Evert if the wearer laces tightly, he doe, not draw in the corset unduly over the ribs, ;tnd pressure at the back and sides is less harmful And more easily endured. Much of the unsightly prominence below the waist was really created by the corset, which drew in to the front waist line, and a corset of the same siza as usually worn, if it has a straight front, is immensely more comfortable, and gives even a smaller effect than the old style. Black silk dresses have come in again' and are enriched with all sorts of stitchings and pipings and tuckings. One of them has the skirt arranged in longitudinal tucks, about three-quarters of an inch wide. On each of these was laid a very open insertion of black guipure, which gave a very handsome appearance to the gown. The yoke was guipure over dull black silk, and the sleeves had several band of th 3 guipure on tucks running from shoulder to wrist.

Your loving JENNIE.

THE MOST NUTRITIOUZ.

E P PS'S

GFATEFUL-COMFORTING.

COCOA

3REAKFA,ST--SUPPER,

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Queen Consort has almost completed the at paint- meats in her new Household, and the Dtinheme of Bur- elecuti instill to be *stress of the Robes. There was some doubt whether there would be a Mietress of the Robes, but it seems to have been decided that Her Majesty is to have all the hottours, as if she were a Sovereign reigning In her own right. Another favourite of Queen Victoria's who is included in the new Household, is the Countess of Antrim, who retains her position as a Lady of the Bedchamt er. Miss Knellys, who has so Img been the intimate friend of the Queen. and her daughters, n•w becomes a Woman of the Bedchamber, and the Maids of Honour are Miss Mary Dyke, rhe lion. Sylvia Edwardes the Hon. Derothy Vivian, and the Hon Vine' Queen Victoria had, during her long reign, hat ten Mistresses of the Robes. The first lady to be annointed to this. the highest position a woman can hold in the Royal fleusieheld. was Harriet, Duchess of Sutherland, I who was three times Mistress of the Robes, next came Charlotte, Duchess of Buccleuch; the others being Anne, I Duchess of Atholl ; the Dachess of Manchester ; the Duchess of Wellington ; Elizabeth. Duchess of Areyll ; Anne, Duchess of Sutherland ; the Iluchesa of Bedford ; and the Duchess of Roxburg. In 1885. the Duchess of Buccleuch was made Mistress of the Robes, and she was again filling this important post, at the time of the late Queen's death. The Duchess of Bucoleucb, who is quite the most exclusive woman ir society, comes of a ducal family, noted for its great beauty—that of the Duke of Abercern Her various entertainments at Montagu HouQe, White- hall, to which an invitation is deemed an honour indeed are magnificent, and one of the greatest features of the London season. Montagu House is built upon the site, of the old residence of the Dukes of Montagu, whirsh was demolished some forty years ago, to make place foe the present structure. In style it is Italian, and has a line hall and staireaae, and the picture gallery contains many valuable cenoaees by the old treaters. The picturesque terrace rarden overlooking the Embankment and the Thames, is the admiration of all privileged to see it. The new Bishop of London is generally considered to be a High Churchman though broad-minded and inde- pendent in his religious views. Dr Winnineton berm, is full of energy and earnestness in hie work, and no clout these qualities have had something to do with his erornotion to the arduous position of the Bishopric of London. It was thrueht that either the Bish,p of Winchester or the Bishop of Rochester would succeed the late Dr Creighton, hut it may he doubted whether Dr Davidson, or Dr Talbot, would have the physical strength to cope with the duties. Dr Ineram, on the other hand is a comparatively young man, his age being but 43, and he is a bachelor. It is long since London bad a bachelor Bishop. and some surprise has been ex. Pressed at the selection of Dr Ingram on this FPS e It bairerver, this is an objection, it is capab'e of being remedied. and as a Suffragon Bishop in the Diocese of London, he has already displayed the qualities which are required, in his now more responsible position. Once again there are rumours to the effect that Lady Ulric' Duncembe, whose brief engagement to rhs Winnington-Ingram somewhat startled Society two or three years ago, is about Co enter a charitable insetution as Lady Superior. Lady Ulrica is the youneest of Lord and Lady Faversham's daughters. The eldest of We beautiful quartet—The Duchess of L'ineter—died of consumption some few years hack, in the eery zenith of her attractiveness, but Lady Helen Vincent whose beauty dazzles all who see it, and Lady Cynthia Graham, are happily still with us. Unlike her sisters. Lady Critics cares nothing for fashion or Society, her t ast( a lying in quite opposite directions. The Free Churches have been holding some great meet- ings in the Park Hall at Cardiff, which is stated to be the largest hall in Wales. No lees than a thousand repre- sentatives have attended from the Free Churches, of England and Wales. The Federation report makes some complaints against what is called " Angliean Tyranny." It does not bring a general charge of persecution against the Anglican Church, bet the Council says "there is no doubt whatever that in the rural districts the Free Churhes are regarded by the bigoted sacerdotalists as weeds and pests, which must be rooted out and dee- treyed." and the report gives several specific instances of spiteful oppression displayed towards the Nonconform- ist's!. The general reports were such as to afford to the Federatien reasons for congratulation. no fewer than 87 new Councils having been opened during the year. , Outdoor exercise is happily thought more and more of, as time goes on, and at the present moment that best of all exercise—walking—is in great favour. Given a proper amount of outdoor life, unlimited tubbing,plenty Of fruit, fresh and stewed; also green vegttablee ; and little in the way of greasy foods, cakes. pastry, sweets. and all highly seasoned dishes,a good complexion should ha our let. But to resume. Tennis promises to find fewer devotees this corning season, croquet having in great measure taken its place in our affections. To all but the quite young, and very energetic, the latter is by far the more desirable game; and from an artistic paint of view there is nn comparison. Few women look their best when heated and dishevelled, a state of affairs ;- possible when croquet is concerned The new cretonne appliqr es would seem to have a considerable future before them. On linen and cotton gowns they look particularly well, and the process comes quite within the scope of the home wetter. The most delightful schemes of colour and arrangement lie this way, and the Outlay need only be infinitesimal. Appliql:ea of brocade are also well to the fore, being used on plain soft silks, with excellent effect. All soft fabrics are the vogue nowadass, wiry, assertive materials finding no place in Fashion's van. Even silks are dressed limp, the glace and Japanese varieties for example, once so crisp and fittn, being as limp and soft as the rest. Since the Duchess of Marlborough first set forth the charms of a large Charles I collar, in deep vandykos, there has been quite a run upon this particular accessory, both in guipure and Irish point. That these collars are a desirable addition to • toilette. goes without saying, I since they induce the shuolders to assume the droop now deemed so eminently correct. The edge of many a fashionable tunic is being finished in vandyker, to bear out the idea, clouds; of billowy flounces issuing from the maces between the points. Such is the ptesent variety in the style of trimming, and shape of headeear, that there in no pessihle excuse for a woman settling upon an unbecoming idea. Vet how often is one's sense of " fitness " outraged in this was'. There in. for instance, the p'ump. round-checked damsel, who will pin her faith and tiff, ctions upon a tcque Smaller than her face,and again therein the woman with a long thin face. who persists in wearing either an enormous bat, or a tiny toque. Then as to trimmings, generally speaking the low, all round style is net becom- ing, ard the upright trimmings are not permissible.—A little tact however is all that is necessary to combine the becoming with the fashionable. The toque or hat can he 'raised well on the left side, thus giving the desired height, or the trimmings so arranged as to give this effect, without being of the upstanding order. If we seek them ways may always be found in which we can adapt a mode to our eenecial requiremente and there- in lies the secret of the really well-dressed among us. The taking of the census, on the last day of the month, is no doubt necessary. but many women have more than a sentimental objection to disclosing their see—and to them, the census paper is an abomination. This is particularly the case witk, women working and living in large drapery or other business establishments. When the paper is passed round it ;s not pleasant to those who have long passed the "atenei g still slaae" to have all their secrets become common knosvlesge. More- over the discloeure might jeopardise the position of an assistant with her employer. We are often told that men ere " too old at forty," but women become " too old." long before that age, and it can easily he under- stood that the census paper becemes a terror, and a i erent ncentive to he untruthful, to many women when their means of livelihood may depend upon it. •• MADGE.

RUBY LOVEDAY.

BY SILAS K. 110CIC.ING,

Author of "The Strange Adventures of Israel • rendray," "The Day of Recompense," "God's Outcast," "In Spite of Fate," "Her Bonny," &c.

CHAPTER

A. STRANGE*.

"We will not from the helm to sit and weep: But keep our course, though the rough winds say no."

John Pentyre-read Mr. Bice's lttter in absolute• silence. He. would not blame anybody, not even himself. He had done the best he knew, and if he had made a mistake he would bear the consequences ' without .whining. Justin and Dorothy went out into the orchard and talkedfthe matter over in the shadow of the trees. Mrs. Pentyre retired to her bedroom and eased her heart in a flood of tears. "What shall we do, Justin ? " Dorothy said, with a. little catch in her breath. "Stay where we are," he replied. "Hadn't we better go out quietly ? "No • let•him put us out by force. It will be an object lesson to the parish." "I am afraid it won't do the parish any good." "Perhaps not. The people have become such serfs that they scarcely any longer resent the brutal rule of wealth and monopoly. Still, we should be cowards if we yielded tamely." "Do you think he will resort to extreme measures ? " Dorothy asked, timidly. "I've not a doubt of it. He would enjoy nothing better than battering the house down about our ears." _

"And will you let him do it ? " he likes. You and mother can go to some safe plat* and father and I will keep the castle." Dorothy's eyes sparkled. "Nay," she said, •"I will stay and see the fun." John Pentyre, however, had no idea of any such resistance. On the ' following morning, without telling anyone of his intentions, he made his way by the shortest cuts to the steward's house. Mr. Bice was at breakfast, and was in no humour to hurry o'er his,nteal. John sat on the edge of a ChM* in a shabby little room and twirled his hat between his hands. A cheap Swiss clock ticked loudly on the mantelpiece. Every now and then John glanced up to see how time was progressing, and sighed as he did so. Surely fingers never moved so Slowly on a dial before. Mr. Bice stalked in at length without ceremony, picking his teeth with.a quill. "Oh, good-morning, he said, abruptly, as though he had not the remotest idea who was waiting for him. "Good morning," John said, nervously. "I've come to see you about that notice you sent last evening:" e. "Plain enough, wasn't it?" "Yes, it was plain enough. I want you to take it back." "You do, eh?" and Mr. Bice smiled good humouredly. "Don't like the prospect, eh ? " and his smile becathe broader, "Own yourself beaten, eh ?" and he laughed outright.. "I have bien unable to find the deeds," John said, plaintively. Mr. Bice's few changed in a moment. "You never expected to find them," he said, angrily. "People can't And things that have no existence." "But they existed once," John said, humbly. "You .try to think so, no doubt. But the truth is you and your- .rascally ancestors have been living for generations on stolen property:" ' *" `!You are. safe, no doubt, in abusing dead people," John answered, with a quick IlaSh in his eye; "the/ are unable to defend themselves." Mr. Bice winced and set his teeth tightly together. • "For myself," John went on ."I have always believed that the title was good. Unfortunately, however, I cannot produce it. For that matter you cannot produce any title to it, either." "But we can," snapped Mr. Bice. "Then it is a bogus one," John said, with spirit, "and am too weak to contest it. In cases of this kind it is the strong who win. I've come to recdgnise the inevitable or I shouldn't have come here this morning." "Well, and what do you want ? " growled the steward. "I want,to keep the farm, arid for the sake of it I'll give up,,Endilloe." "No doubt of it. I thought you would come to your senses some time. It's a pity you've been so long over it." "I've tried to do what is right," John said, 'humbly. "You have, eh ? Then all I can say is that your notions of-right are somewhat peculiar," and Mr. Bice chuckled. John flushed, but did not speak. Mr. Bice walked to the window, and made more ostentatious use of his tooth-pick. On the whole he was enjoying the , interview. To see John Pentyre humble and at his feet was like nectar to him. "You hardly expect me, of course," he said at .length, "to offer you the old terms?" "Why not?." John questioned, timidly. "Why not? Well, the reasons are too numerous to mention. I've proved my case since then. The element of doubt has disappeared. You own your- self that you occupy property that you have no right to." _ _ _

===== 0002601_19010315_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
own, to nothing of the kind," John said,

hotly. "What ? " and Mr. Bice faced round with a blaze of passion in his eyes. "I've every moral right to the place," John continued. "I've the right of long possession. But Endifloe is of very little use to me without the farm. You know that. That's where you've got . the upper hand of me." . "Ind what is more, we intend to keep it," was ' the reply. "You have been defiant, sic, and even impudent, now yol: will take. the consequences." "You mean-- "I mean exactly what I said in my letter yester- day. I am not in the habit of changing my mind two or three times a day. Now go home and get ready to quit, for if you are in the place an hour beyond the time, by Heaven you'll rue it!" "Ind this is your final answer ? " "448, "Then' you'll rue it also," and John rose suddenly to his feet and made for the door. His.. gentle eyds were blazing, his lips were white with. anger. Mr. Bice had supplied him with the fillip he needed. By the time he reached Endilloe he was more eager than Justin to fight the battle to the death. In one of the nearer fields he stumbled across Nathan and told him how matters stood. "And be you agoi,n' to stick out?" Nathan asked. "To the last gasp," was the reply. "Then I'm yer man," said Nathan. "Endilloe ain't got three-foot walls and oaken doors for nothin': My stars we'll have a time of it." "You'll stand by me, Nathan ?" "To the laast, master." John-grasped his hind's hard hand and walked 'on in silence. That evening John discussed with Justin and Dorothy plans for defence. His good spirits had quite returned. Indeed, he was the most cheerful of the family. "We'll have some fun out of the business, at any rate," he said. "And if they get Endilloe it won't be worth much to them." "Blit‘ve shall have to leave the dear old place in the end," Dorothy said, plaintively. "I won't even say that," John answered. "There's no knowing what will happen." During • the next day or two John and Justin made rparations for a lengthened siege. They knew t e temper of Mr. Bice, and Lord 'Tregeagle was as reckless as his steward when his blood was" up. Mr: Bice was careful to omit no legal formality, and in every case acted on the assitnap. I ion that Lord Tregeagle's claim was indisputable. Joii'? sent him word that he disputed Lord Tregeagle's claim, and that he should defend his home if needs be with his life. That any person seen about the place would be regarded as trespasser and wonld be treated accordingly. That Endilloe was well furnished with fire-arms and other means of defence, that he and his fainily had counted the costs and were prepared to abide by the consequences. , Mr. Bice read John's letter with knitted brows and a troubled light in his eyes. He had made a mistake in estimating his charac'er. He had expected a flash of temper now and then, but he had not anticipated any real resistance.. He was sorry now that he had not accepted John's offer. ; To evict people by force was always an unpleasant task; besides, it would rouse the slumbering antagonism of every man in the parish. . But he had gone too far to draw back. He would have to carry the matter through whatever the

consequences might be. To own himself defeated would make him the laughing-stock of the whole district and set every other small freeholder at defiance. So he sent him word that he would give him till six o'clock on Thursday evening to clear out in a quiet and orderly fashion. If, however, he was not out of the house bag and baggage by that time a sufficient force of police would be on hand to evict him in a manner that might not be altogether pleasant. To this John sent no reply, and so matters remained until the fateful day. On the evening previous John and his two chil- dren were discussing events as they had discussed them a hundred times before. They talked quietly and without passion, while the twilight of the long summer day deepened rapidly. Every now and then a long pause intervened, for each had thoughts which even the nearest of kin might not share. Dorothy thought of Torn Pendarvis, and wondered why he kept silent so long. Justin thought of Ruby. He could not help it. She might be the wife of another; but that could not alter the fact that she had stolen his heart, and he honestly believed that he would never love twain. During one of those pauses the door opened, and Mrs. Pentyre came into the room. "Dorothy," she said, "there is a man at the back door who wants to see you. He does not give his name, but he says he has a message fc,r you." "Wants to see me?" Dorothy questioned, turn- ing quickly round. "What is he like?" "Well, I couldn't see his face very plainly, for it's darker out in the porch than here, but he looks quite an elderly man." Dorothy hesitated, and looked at her brother. "I don't expect he will eat you," Justin said, with a laugh. "You had better go and see what he wants." "He looks to be very respectably dressed," Mrs. Pentyre interposed. "I expect he's on some begging expedition," Dorothy said, with a laugh. "However, I will go and interview him." Mrs. Pentyre took Dorothy's chair, and con- versation started afresh. Dorothy found the stranger sitting on a bench in the shadow of a large thorn-tree a few paces from the door. He raised his brown wideawake hat as she came towards him, and she saw that his hair as well as his beard was quite white. I3ut he made no attempt to rise. "You are Miss Dorothy Pen—Pentyre ? " he questioned, in a low, husky voice, as though he were suffering from a cold. "Yes," she said, "that is my name. Did you wish to see me ? " "I have a message fer Miss Dorothy Pentyre," he answered, slowly, and in the same husky tones, "and I am to deliver it only to her." "A message from whom?" she questioned, doubtfully. "From Toni Pendarvis," he answered, slowly. "Poor Tom, he never tired talking about you." "Then you know him ?" she questioned, eagerly. "I knew him once, miss. Knew him well." "But where and when ?" "In the Transvaal, miss. We were comrades and, as a rule, we cared for no other company." "And did he get on?" she asked, eagerly. "That is, did he work hard, and try his best?" "He did, miss; lie worked almost night and day. He was always something of a schemer, so he told me, and he invented a machine for boring rock and another for crushing ore. But it nearly killed him. It isn't safe to burn the candle at both ends, miss." "Oh, I knew he would get on if he had the chance," she said, clasping her hands tightly together; "but why did he not send word? Why did he not let me know?' "People do not get on in a day," was the answer. "And then he fell ill, and when he lay at the point of death, I promised him I'd come and see you if I ever visited the Old Country again, and tell you that he never wavered in his love for you —no, not for a moment." "Then—then—he' s—he ' s--dead " she gasped, leaning against the porch for support. "No, don't say that, 'miss—don't say that. There was still life in him when I left the Transvaal ; and if I could only send him word that you loved him still, I believe it would do him more good than all the medicine in the world." "Oh, how could he ever doubt me I" she wailed. "He thought you were like the rest, miss—that you had lost faith in him." "But I never did lose faith in him—never, never," she cried. "He only wanted an oppor- tunity. He was cleverer than all his companions put together." "Then your message is reconciliation?" he said. "Reconciliation ? " she cried. "Oh, no; that is not the right word. We never quarrElled. Tom was proud; he thought I was dissatisfied, and so he went away. Tell him I have never changed." "I will, miss." "And tell.him—oh, tell him that I want him to come home. He will understand that." "He will be very stupid if he don't, miss." "But letters are so slow. Could you not tele- graph to him? Oh, if he only knew, lam sure he would not stay away." "Let me think, miss," and the old man leant his elbows upon his knees and buried his face in his hands. And when he raised his head again, the wideawake hat lay on the ground, and a young man sat in his pilace under the tree. CHAPTER XXII. TIIE EVE OF THE BATTLE. "Love has so filled my heart, That there is no room for fear,"

Dorothy darted forward with a. glad cry: "0 Tom, Toni !" and the next moment they were clasped in each other's arms. For a while a sweet rain of kisses put an end to speech. Then Dorothy drew herself away and said: "But how could you,

Tom ?" "I couldn't resist the temptation, darling," he said, with a laugh. "I had read about it being done in novels, and I thought I. would like to make the experiments° though, candidly, I did not think it would succeed." "And it wouldn't have succeeded, only it was so nearly dark." "Exactly, that was part of the little scheme." really

"And did you redly think, you naughty boy, that I had changed?" "I did not know what to think, Dorothy. A lot may happen in three or four years." But it's a poor love that won't endure a inuch longer silence than that." "Yes, I know. But I feared you would think I was an utter failure, and so put me out of your thoughts. Such things have happened, you know. And sometimes new loves have driven out the old." "And if I had changed, Torn?" "Then I should have gone away quietly, and no one would have known that I had been. That was say little plot, Dorothy." 'And you are glad that you had to reveal yourself ?' And for answer he kissed her again and again. Two or three minutes later Justin came upon the scene, much wondering what had become of his sister. Dorothy was telling Tom of the notice they had to "quit, and what preparations they were making in face of siege ; and so interested were they both, that they did not hear the door open, and were unconscious of the fact that Justin stood in the porch staring at them. As a matter of fact, Justin was too astonished to speak. It had grown too dark for him to distin- guish faces; but Dorothy's voice was unmistakable, nor was it so dark but that he could see she sat close to the stranger, and, what was more, that he had his arm about her waist. The proper thing to have done would have been to have coughed. Ile had read novels enough to understand that. In books, properly-conducted people always cough when they wish to make their presence known. But Justin was too astonished, not to say indignant, to act in any such considerate fashion. As soon as he recovered himself, lie exclaimed in a tone of righteous indignation: "Dorothy, what is the meaning of this?" Instantly she was on her feet. "0 Justin," she cried, coming towards h'm with outstretched hands, "Tom has come home!" "Torn?" he questiot ed, doubtfully. "You mean Tom Pendarvis?" "Why, of course! What other Tom could I mean ? " "Exactly; there is no other Tom, is there?" and the next moment the two young men had clasped hands, and were looking into each other's faces. "I am glad to see you home again, Toni," Justin said, heartily; "I am, upon my soul I " "But not so glad as 1 am to be at home," was

the reply. "But when did you come?" "I landed this morning at Plymouth." "Ah, been abroad, have you?' "I have had three years in Africa." "And have you come home to stay P" "I hope so. Do you think your father will see me ? " "I dr,n't know. You have come at a good time." "I might be of service. Defending one's house against attack is common enough in Africa, and I have had plenty of experience."

"Then you are just the man we want. Old Bice vows that if we don't go out quietly, he will evict us by force; and I believe he will try." "I hope you will stay and help us," Dorothy fnterposed at this point. "Oh, I hope you will.' "I will, if your father will let me," was the reply. "That we can easily find out," Justin said. "Dorothy and I will go and send him to you." In the hall they met John coming to see what had become of them; so, telling him that he was wanted outside, they went in and waited the result with as much patience as they could command. Justin and his mother kept up a desultory con- versation, but Dorothy was too excited to talk. Mrs. Pentyre was curious to know who the strange man was that had kept them so long; but the only answer she could get was: "We have sent father to find out."

The old eight-day clock ticked steadily on, and it had grown so dark that they could not see each other's faces; but no attempt was made to light the lamp. Dorothy pleaded that she loved the darkness of a summer's night, and that it was not so dark but that they could see to talk. _

So the minutes sped on, and Dorothy's heart kept beating faster and faster. She knew that her cheeks must be burning bright red, and that her eyes would be unusually brilliant. She would not have the lamp lighted just now on any account. She tried to take part in the intermittent talk between her mother and Justin, but words would not come. She was compelled to be silent even against her will. At length her quick ear detected a footstep, then the door opened. She thought her heart had stopped. Was her father coming back alone? She placed her hand to her side and held her breath. That was her father's footstep, and his only. No, there was another. Tom was coming with him. It was all right. Her heart bounded on again,' but the colour faded suddenly out of her cheeks. She rose tremblingly and wont and lighted the lamp, her ears humming strange music all the while. She felt as light as air. All the darkness and oppression had gone out of her heart. It was nothing to her that they were threatened with evic- tion. Tom had come home and had made his peace with her father. It must be so, or he would not be coming with him into the house. - -

She turned from lighting the lamp, and her eyes met her lover's; they were full of light and love. She did not want him to tell her anything then. She knew all.

Mrs. Pentyre was staring at the new arrival in wondering surprise. This was not the stranger she had seen at the back door. But nobody seemed inclined to offer any further explanations. "Give the boy welcome, mother," John said, with a curious inflection in his voice. "It's all right." And Mrs. Pentyre, who had always been fond of the light-hearted young fellow, put her arms round his neck and kissed him.

Tom's eyes ran full of tears in a moment. It was a welcome such as he had never expected, and it made him think how much he had lost in the early des t h of his own mother.

Justin put an end to the general embarrassment by suggesting that supper should be brought in, and Mrs. Pentyre hurried off into the kitchen followed by Dorothy. The three men saw little of bed that night. Tom's quick eye and larger experience saw a dozen things that needed doing if the house was to be safely garrisoned, and when morning broke it pre- sented a strange appearance. All the lower windows had been securely boarded up, while the doors were protected by stout deal planks, indeed, it was quite evident that the defenders meant business, and the news quickly ran through the neighbourhood that the Pentyres intended to defy Lord Tregeagle and the law.

-- - Tom's great regret was that there was not a garden-hose in the house. There was such a mag- nificent water supply that he thought it a pity it could not be brought into use.

"A good dousing of cold water," he,said to John, 'is better than grape shot for dispersing a crowd." But John shook his head. Now that his blood was up, he was strongly disposed to give the attacking party a welcome of a somewhat warmer character. After breakfast, Tom went in search of Nathan. "Drive over to Trelford at once," he said, "and fetch back twenty yards of rubber tubing, also nozzle and rose, and a few yards of copper wire." "What be 'em for ? " Nathan asked. "Oh, I'm going to manufacture a garden-hose," was the reply. "It will come in handy also for swilling out the stable and many other things." Nathan scratched his head for several seconds then walked slowly away, and a quarter of an hoar later was driving rapidly in the direction of Trelford. As the day wore away the excitement at Endilloe became intense. No detachment of troops waiting for the first sound of battle was ever more on the alert. That the conflict in which they were engaged was to all appearances destined to end in disaster, did not detract in the least from their determination to carry it through to the bitter end. They could but lose. They were bound to lose in any case, and so they would lose fighting. _lt would be a satisfaction in after years to remember that they had resisted wrong to the best of their ability; that they had not flung away their birth- right as though it were a worthless thing. In the end they had no doubt they would see their furniture thrown pell mell into the garden, and would find themselves standing outside the house that had so long sheltered them. Such things went on constantly in the British Isles. Not only in the country, but in the chief cities. But a few weeks before they had read in the news- papers, how a dozen families had been evicted in a poor part of London, and how their furniture had been stacked in the streets for days on the stretch, and how the poor people sat watching night after night by their household gods. Such martyrdoms might do good in some far-off and roundabout way. It was necessary that light should be let. in upon the conscience of England on some social and economic questions and to do this it was needful that someone should suffer. As John Pentyre sat brooding over his wrongs and waiting for the attack, he was conscious that a change was slowly taking place within him. He had be,in a most loyal subject ,a strict Conservative, a strong upholder of the Constitution, a devout worshipper of the British aristocracy, an implicit believer in the right of those who had, to rule over those who had not. Nor had these beliefs been weakened by anything that his neighbours or even his own ancestors had suffered. That the mining industry was crippled by heavy dues, that the small freeholder had practically disappeared from their midst, that the leaseholders were daily wasting their strength and substance for the enrichment of others, were not matters that had troubled him at all. But somehow now, when his turn had come to be the pipkin between the iron pots, he seemed to see things in a somewhat different light. He did not appreciate being a modern Naboth, the loss of his vineyard touched him to the quick, and the strength of the giants seemed to be more cruelly used than before.

Justin shared very largely his father's thoughts, but Tom and Dorothy had much pleasanter things to think of. Dorothy moved about like one in a dream. It seemed almost too good to be true that Tom should be home again; and every time they met, her face would light up with a fresh accession of joy. Busy as they were all kept. Tom and Dorothy managed to snatch a good many moments together when no one else was near. They had so much to talk about, so many plans to discuss, so much lost time to make up, that they were often sublimely unconscious of the fact that there were such people as Lord Tregeagle and his steward in the world. The loss of Endilloe seemed a comparatively_ small matter to Dorothy now that she had found Tom. At a quarter to six Nathan came to the ba:.k door with the tidings that Mr. Bice and two c, tstables were corning up the road .in the direction of Endilloe. Nathan was at once admitted and the door made fast again. Tom had completed his hose arrange- ment, and satisfied himself that it was in good working order. There was nothing to be done now but to'calmly await the issue. Mr. and Mrs. Pentyre, JuAin and Dorothy took uD positions at the upstairs windows. Tom and Nathan remained downstairs. A few minutes before six Mr. Bice was seen to reach the garden-gate, accompanied by the constables, and followed at P considerable distance by a crowd of men, w mien, and children, who evidently intended to see the fun. At sight of the barricaded house, Mr. Bice paused, and held a hurried consultation with his companions, while the crowd came perceptibly nearer. Mr. Bice evidently felt that Isis force was not sufficient, particularly as there were clear mani- festations that he had a hostile crowd at his back. Nevertheless, his first duty was clear, and that was to go to the house and demand in the name of the law that John Pentyre- and his family quietly and at once vacate it. If they still refused, it would be easy to get together a larger force of constables

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS. --- Important Meeting. A meEting of this Board was held in the Board Room It the Workhouse on Wednesday morning. Mr A J Vim" presided, and there were also present : Mrs Clay, ev J Riddette, D Patterson, Messrs Henry Thornton, Davies, T B Malkin, William Houghton, T Halton,T H therqt. A Jollny, 0 Pilling, E L Bent. A Brooks, J il:^nuul•with J C Sutton (clerk), C II Ward (master)and Wright (Pada ate Schools). THE ASSESSMENTS COMMITTEE AND THE CLERK. IF YOU HAD PLENTY OF MONEY.

THANKS to your great generosity in sending me that five thousand pounds I now have plenty of money in my pocket ; and am free from the worries and trials of poverty, from which I had suffered so long. There ! a you had reason to write to a man in that way what a thing it would be, the money, and your gratitude to him who furnished it in the day of your dire need.

The writer avdl make no silly pretence of not caring for money. He does care for it and so do all men and women of sound minds. For money represents about everything in this world worth having, except good health ; and in a way money has a deal to do with that. "Thanks to your wonderful medicine," writes Mr. W. J. Seuffine, of 35, Willow Grove, Burley, Leeds, "I am now enjoying gcod health after suffering a long time frcrn indignation, " In the Spring of 1897 I found my health giving way. My skin turned dull and sallow and thel whites of my eyes actually got as yellow as an old cold coin. Of courae you will understand what ailed me ; it was biliousness, one of the commonest and meanest of all the ailments that make a man feel like getting down on all fours and doing a crawl.

"My right side, down where the liver i,was so so re

I couldn't bear to touch it. When I ate a bit to keep myself from starving, I had such pains in the chest and back I wished I had let food alone; and if there had been a stone weight tied against my chest I couldn't have felt much worse in that region than I did as it was. " The taste in my mouth was horrible ; it almost made me sick. All my strength seemed leaving me,and when I got home at night from work I was so weary and fagged I ba I to lie down ; and now and then I was obliged to leave off work for days. "Slime of m 7 neighbours thought I was going off in a consumption, I got so weak and so thin, and used to spit up such a lot of phlegm when I coughed. I spent pounds on doctors and their medicines and, but for Mother Seigel's Syrup, I don't know what would have become of me. I had been ill fifteen months an am sure I couldn't have stood it much longer. " The very first bottle of S4gel's Syrup gave me great relief and the improvement went on until I was strong and well again. Now I have a good appetite and what. ever I eat agrees with me. " I owe my present health to Seigel's Syrup and no amount of money could have given me the comfort and contentment which resulted from the use of that splen- did medicine. You are at liberty to publish my state- ment." W J Scuffing, April 9th, 1900.

MAN DECAPITATED ON THE CHESHIRE LINES AT WARRINGTON.

Supposed Suicide.

. . About eight o'clock, on Friday a shocking dis- covery was made on the Cheshire Lines Railway near Marshhouse-lane Bridge by Edward Howard, one of the employes. He was walking along the line when he was horrified to find on the down road a man decapitated. The man appears to have been lying with his neck across the metals, and his body in the six foot- way A passing train nad severed the head from the body in guillotine fashion. No other portion of his body was injured and the circum- stances seem to point to suicide. He was removed , on an ambulance to the Central Station at Warring- ton where Dr Nuttsll was called in and formally pronounced life extinct. On being searched a bottle of whiskey and some money were found in his pockets. There Was nothing upon him to lead to his identification.

===== 0002601_19010315_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
The Inquest.

No Identification.

At noon on Tuesday Mr 9 Brighouse, vanity Coroner, conducted an inquest at the Police Station. relative to the death of A man unknown whose decapitated body was found on the Cheshire Lines Railway near Marsh- house-lane bridge on Friday night last week. Mr Bimp- SOn represented the railway company. The body which was stilt unidentified was described by tea police as follows :—About 45 years of age, 51t. Bins in height, medium build, dark brown hair, heavy brown moustache, thin features and long nose; dressed in a dark grey jacket, dark serge vest, grey tweed trousers, blue cotton shirt with white stripes, white flannel singlet, blue reeffi sr with white spots, brown worsted stockings, dark cap, whiel is greasy, lace-up boots of respectable appearance ; in his pockets, tw‘.. purses, El. 23 Olin cash, a white pocket-handkerchiet, and a tailor's thimble. The first witness called was Edward Howard, a goods checker on the Cheshire Lines Railway at Warrington, and living at 8 Station-row, Padgate, who said that at eight o'olock on Friday fast he was proceeding along the line in the direction of ,Padgate.Near to the MarshEiouse- lane bridge he saw the body of the deceased on the em- bankment. The night was dark. He ran to the signal box and obtained a lamp. He returned and examined the body. The head was severed from the body, and was in the four-foot of the down main line. He ob- tained assistance, and the body was removed to the Central Station, and afterwards to the mortuary. The Coroner : The man had evidently beer. run over ? Witness : Yes. Sergeant Bennett said that when he received informa- tion of the discovery he went to the Central Station, and had the body removed from there to the mortuary. He described the body and clothing as given above. The Coroner : You found no writing of any descrip- tion upon it ? Witness Nothing at all. The Coroner There was no mark on the clothing ? Witness : No, sir. Detective Barlow, the Coroner's officer, said he had made inquiries, and hart ascertained thatthedetcee Baseady, about half-past six on Friday evening, entered h Horse Inn, Winwick-street, and called for a pint of beer, which he drank. He also bought * Pint of whiskey, and I 'took it away with him. He was a perfect stranger at the hruse. The bottle, containing some of the whiskey, was found upon the body. He had sent a description of the deceased to 35 townships, but the body had not been identified. The jury returned an open verdict. The Coroner afterwards asked Mr Simpson if it did not look like a case of suicide. Mr Simpson said there was very little doubt that decerved had committed suicide. The deceased's shoulder was close to the rail and the head was severed from the body as though it had brea cut with a pair of shears. No marks were found on any of the engines but it was probable that an express ran over the de- ceased.

"IS THIS A_ SQANDAL P"-

NOI

The Magistrates Defended.

It is evidently the determination of some people to provide Warrington with a scandal, even if it lets to be a manufactured and imported article. We have become accustomed to the catch-halfpenny efforts of an outside newspaper to drag Warrin;ton's name through the mire, and have got into the habit of treating the exaggerated eff asions emanating from that quarterwith contempt. But the business of scandal-mongering , seems to have spread and established itself nearer home, for only last Saturday our Conservative contemporary published a lengthy article reflecting on the local magisterial bench, and which bore the attractive heading "Is this a scandal ?" Though the title takes the form of a query, the article itself sought to make the existence of an actual scandal self-evident. What was said to be wrong is this. The magistrates require their clerk to furnish them with a written notice of any licensing prosecution prier to the headag of the case, and this custom has been in vogue for some years. Consequently there is always , a lull benchlwhen any such case is before the court. From these two simple facts our contemporary makes deductions which are most decidedly u i lair to the magistrates, and which reveal the want of proper enquiry tied due consideration on the part of the writer ere he put pen to the paper. the sug- gestion is that a majority of the magistrates have " a special desire to adjudicate, in licensing cases and not in the interests of justice. Q.iestions are asked with alarming fluency, such as : " What can be the object in sending out the notice ?" ; Is the motive which prompts it an honourable one?"; " Why should the publican be treated differently from other people? "—and so on ad infinitum. In fact there is more query than answer in the whole article, and yet the assertion is boldly made that the Warrington scandal can give the recent Mitt- cheater magisterial scandal a good start and beat it. Tbe whole thing is sadly distorted and a perversion of the truth. There is no " scandal " whatever, though there once was, and the instructieo to the magistrates' clerk which is now regarded so suspic- iously, was devised as the safeguard of justice end has ever since admirably fulfilled the purpose. Toe orig nator of it was one of Warrington's most hon- oured townsmen, Mr Frederick Monks, J.P., whose name alone is a warranty for the honesty and fair- ness of anything with which he is connected. Will it be believed that he is responsible for the " scandal " which our contemporary so unctu usly exposes?We have interviewed Mr Monks and found him willing to give the fullest possible information concerning 90 serious a matter. He asserts—and we can well believe him—that prior to the issuing of the notice re licensing cases, certain magistrates used to make a point of attending the police court when a publican was on his al, with the almost invariable result that Boniface was acquitted, no matter how black the case appeared against him. The Chief Constable was in despair, and it was to strengthen his hands and t 3 see that justice , was properly administered that Mr' Monks took the public-spirited course he did in causing notice to be always given to the bench of licensing prosecutions. The result has been, ever since, that a thoroughly representative and impar- tial tribunal has administered the law affecting publicans and the liquor traffic% Convictions have been secured and offenders punished who would, in former times, haverleft the court laughing up their sleeves at the charming efficacy of having "friends in court." This is the whole" scandal" correctly detailed. We think the magistrates will at once be cleared of any such imputations as were cast upon them a week ago, and supported in ei- twin they have taken up on so imp..,-,ant and delicate a question. It is in the interests of tutb the public and the publican—for bias does not lean all one way—that the notice should continue to be issued, and unlike our cnitemporavy we do not " hope to see the resolution rescinded at the earliest possible moment."

===== 0002601_19010315_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
"Truth's" Comment.

Last week I commented upon tne practice at Warrington of giving a special notice to every magistrate whenever a licensing prosecution is com- ing on at the Borough Police-court. The " Wei- rington Examiner" now offers the interesting explanation that this practice was actually devised to put an end to a gross scandal. It was formerly found that certain magistrates not on the rota made a point of attending the Police-court whenever a publican was on his trial, " with the almost invariable redulc that Boni- face was acquitted, no matter how black the case appeared against him." The Chief Constable is said to have been "in despair," and it was "to strengthen his hands and to see that justice was properly administered," that the practice of issuing a notice to every magistrate was adopted. This statement cerUtinly gives a different complexion to what at first sight seemed to be a very reprehensible arrangement. If the friends of Mr Bung are whipped up every time he has to answer a summons, it is only right that the whole of the magistrates should be in- formed of such cases. At the same time the fact the, the magistrates as a body have considered it neeessary to take this precaution against the pack- ing of the Court in licensing cases is a pretty com- mentary upon the manner in which some of their number exercise their judicial office. It is a plain admission that there are gentlemen among them who in the opinion of their own colleagues cannot be trusted to administer the law impartially. In licensing cases the tendency to bias is specially noticeable, and it is high time this part of their business, at least, was taken out of the hands of the Great Unpaid.—" Truth."

WARRINGTON. Explosion of a Paraffin Lamp. An inquest was ck,nducted by Mr, BrlghouFe at the Police Station at noon on Tuesday concerning the death, which took place at the Workhouoe Hospital on Monday, of Margaret Bellis (65), of 5, Morris's-court. From the evidence it appeared that the deceased was reading in the kitchen of the house on the night of the 9:h ult, when the paraffin lamp which was on the table, exploded and her clothing caught fire. A neighbour extinguished the flames, and Dr McCarthy attended the deceased until her removal to the Workhouse hospital on the following Monday. She was badly burned about the lower part of the body, and died on Monday afternoon last.—The jury returned a verdict of accidental death.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES,

Bold-street Wesleyan Church.

At Bold-street Wesleyan Church on Sunday the ser- vices were on behalf of the Wesley Guild. There was a good congregation in the morning, when the preacher was the Rev T Bishop, who took for his subject " The World's Ideal Life." Special music was rendered, in- cluding a trio, "Lift thine eyes (Mende ssohn), by the Misses Taylor, Place and Kelly. The anthem, "In- chne thine ear" was also rendered by the choir. Mr F. Hickman ably presided at the organ. The service in the evening was conducted .by the Rev H G Roberts,who spoke on "Strange impressions of faith! al preaching. At the commencemeat of the service the male members of the choir sang the anthem, "Cast thy burden" Men- delseolm), and during the service the choir sang "The Lord is loving" (Dr. Garen.) On Monday evening the members of the Guild ae- sembled in the schoolroom to hear a lecture by the Rev T Bishop, entitled, "China and the Chinese." The lecture was illustrated by limelight views, the lantern b,in, manipulated by Mr Herbert Parkinson. The lecturer ob- served that Caine was 44 times larger than the whole of Great Britain, end the Chinese numbered no fewer than 400 million persons, relatively about one-third of the world's population. If the whole of the inhabitants were to gather together, every third man, woman, and child would be Chinese. If they could stand shoulder to shoulder the Chinese would reach three times round the world and if they were to lie on the ground, head to feet, they would stretch seven or eight times round' If the Chinese were to march four abreast, 100 a minute night and day, it would take them exacdy three years to pass a given place. fhe Great Wall of China was 1,500 miles hog and was wide enough for six horsemen to ride abreast. It was built 246 years before the birth of Christ, and was intended to keep out the Tartars, who invaded China. During the building of the wall it was estimated that half a million persons died. A view of Nanking, the former capital, was given, and it was stated that if two men shrted to ride round the city wall in opposite directions it would take them 14 hours before they met again. But he took that assertion with the usual grain of salt. It was a city of 4,000.000 population, but the Government had been removed to Pekin. There were three religions in Cnina—Con- , fucianism, Taouism, and Buddhism. Views were thrown on the screen illustrating the process of picking tea and 1 preparing it for use in England, and at tne conclu-ion of the lecture Mr Bishop was warmly applauded and heart- ily thanked for his services.

Sermon to Young Men by the Rev. J. Yonge. At Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday evening, a special sermon to young men was preached by the Pastor (the Bev J Y)oge) who took for his text the words " What is your life?" (John 4:h chapter p art of lath verse) and for his sabject "The True Purposes of Life." Be said the question was a personal one, and every person, young and old, should most earnestly con- sider it. Life was a serious thing, for it was responsible ; it waft the most sacred of all imaginable trusts. Lite was a talent to be traded with, a property to b 3 rightly usfd, an opportunity to be diligently improved, a space to be properly filled, a service to be faithfully discharged a school in which to prepare for a life far greater, a race in which to run successfully for the great priz3, a battle in which to be courageous and triumphant. Life, was, therefore, serious because responsible. It was sound wisdom to take life somewhat seriously. By using the word seriously he did not wan, gloomily or sadly. The thought of lives responsible, ai-cl of their personal accountableness to their Maker should ever affect and control them. It should help to moderate their indulgence in youthful pleasures ; it should purify and refine their pleasures ; ennoble them and lift them ud. It was their duty to live thou4htfull7 and care- oraocuhrernature are fully. A passing reference was made to the reprehen- sible habit ot parading Sankey-street by many young people. He was very anxious, he said, that the young p-ople connected with Wycliffe should think about the life. features The tmoesahnoi meaning and ahnadt toe purposeaup re mo f eProceeded not physical and pointed out that the mind was the standard of the man. We were living Its a timif of education, and there was no excuse for ignorance. A Manchester magistrate. speaking at a meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. on the previous Sunday, gave it as his opinion that large numbers of men seemed to feed their minds on football newspapers. Be (Mr. Yonge) was not going to say a single reproachful word to any who found recreation in playing or watching this most popular of English sports, but he hoped he should be forgiven for saying that it often looked to him as if 1 the . energy, enthusiasm, care, time and money which was expended upon it, might easily find a more profitable direction. One could not help wondering very much whe;har a large number of young men could always properly aff ard all the expenditure it involved. One wondered also whether some of the pre- valent practices which invaded and infested the football field did not make that place dangeroua and undesirable for those who did no, wish to be associated with doubt- ful practises and dangerous habits. Intelligent recrea- tion was as lawful an I Csriatian as intelligent work, but life was not mean; to be supremely devoted to it. Recreation should renew the powere of mind and body, and leave no unhappy consequence. But recreation was nat the true purpose of lite ! There was no life so replete with satisfaction as the life of dizcipleship and devotion to the Saviour of the world. Be appealed to I hie bearers to be devoted to Jesus Christ.

FINANCIAL AND: MINING NEWS.

%Vest African Shares.

IA large amount of business is taking place in the West Atrican Market. This is not to be wondered at. when men like Sir J Blundell Maple, Mr Frederick Gordon, and Mr A L Jones, of Liverpool—men who have a great repdtition to lose as well as capitsl—put large sums of money into West African mining ventures. It is certain that they would not invest, as they have done, without first taking every polsible means of getting at the facts, judging the position from a calm business point of view, and believing there are substantial reasons for expecting suc- cess. Considerable interest is being taken in the shares of Wassau Gold Concessions, Limited, which are dealt in on the Loodon Stock Exchange at about 103 pr 53 share fully paid. According to the reports on the Concessions owned by this Company, they sro in the famous Wassail District of the Gold Coast, West Afrcia ; they also state that a rich banket formation exists on the property, which comprises an area of about 24 square miles. The C incessions have heel transferred and registered in the Company's name at Cape Cosst. Toe capital of the Company is only £150,000, of which £50,000 is for working capital. Altogether, the finure of Wassau Gold Concessions, Limited, looks decidedly promising.

Pearson and Knowles, Limited.

Discussing high class Industrial Investments "The Citizen " in its current issue says : The present dulness in cosi and iron share, has pre- vented the merits of the P •arsan and Knowles Coal and Iron Company, Limited, from receiving their due recog- nition. This is partly due to the title of this particular I company being somewhat of a misnomer. Pearson and Karswles are really not producers of iron or steel, but manufacturers of finished merchandise only. Their chief trade is in steel rolled wire, cotton bale biniers especially for the E tspti an market, waggon wheels and axles, Zci. Tne engineering shops cover 40 acres of free- hold land at Warrington and have railway sidings on to the leading railways. In the company's coal leases 30,000,000 tons Of coal are, it is stated, in sight, the yearly output being 1,000.000 tons. The policy of the board has been to write off very large sums each year fer depreciation and reserve, so that the'assets have now been written down to a remarkable extent. The collier- ies, for instance, now Saud in the balance sheet, at £163,000, against which there is a reserve of £140,000. For the twelve months ended June 30th, 1901, the profits on the coal output alone are estimated at £250,000, while the half-year's profits on steel and iron to December 31 last are stated as £125,000. me company derive much advantage from any fall in pig iron, while although its present coal contracts are running at 143 per ton, it could still make a good profit by sellin t it at the pit's mouth at 6i 6d per ton. The capital of the company is £860,000 in 50,000 cumulatiy,, 6 per cent preference shares of £5 each, and 122,000 ordinary shares of £s,each, both fully paid, while there are no debentures, mortgages, or liens on any of the assets. An interim dividend for the half year, at the rate of 10 per cent per annum, the largest allowed by the Articles of Association, has just been declared on the ordinary shares, and it is more than probable that the distribution for the whele year will be much larger. Notwithstanding this distinctly favourable position, however, the shares are still obtainable at 61 and we would specially direct the attention of investors to this verr promising industrial enterprise as one most worthy of their notice."

WARRINGTON LITERARY AND PHILOSOPHICAL SOCIETY.

Election of Officers.

the last meeting of the session of this Society was held on Monday evening in the Masonic Hall, Bold-at., under the presidency of the Rev Morley Stevens,sn.— The PRESIDE:4T said that this being the last meeting of the session a little special business had to be transacted relating to the coining session, He had to propose that Mr J Douglas Buckton be elected president for the forth- coming session. Mr Bucktou had been a member of the society for a great number of years and a member of the council for a considerable period. although by order of rotation he went of last year. He took a real interest in the proceedings of the society,and had been a regular attendant at the meetings, Mr. CHARLES WRITE seconded the motion, and said he bad known Mr. Buckton longer than anyone in that room, and he could give him a god character. (Laugh- ter.) What he did he did well and thoroughly, and now that he was a gentleman at large—(renewed laughter.) and bad no claims upon his time, he noped he would use his ability in resuscitating the society, which had fallen below its usual level. He did not say that they depended upon the President to raise the society from a dead la vel, but there should be enthusiasm on the part of th! townspeople. He was almost hoping that if there was no proper resisense to the wish of the council that the society should he buried for a time in the hope of a more glorious resurrection—Oaughterl—but after the excel- lent attendance that night, and the cheering words of the 'present President, he hoped they would all put their Shoulders to the wheel and do something to restore the Warrington Literary and Philosophical Society to its old position and fame. (Hear, hear, and applause.) The proposition was carri =4:l unanimously. On the moti on of Mr A J JOLLEY, seconded by Mr Or W Monk,, Mr G Carruthers was elected hon. secretary,and Mr William Silcook bon, treasurer of the Society. Mr C S BURGESS moved that the following gentlemen form the Council for the ensuing !year :—Mr May, Mr Houghton, the Rev FE Powell, Mr Young, Mr Haddock, and Mr A Joliev. Mr KENYON seconded the motion, and said he felt sure that all the gentlemen named had the best interests of the society at heart. ' The motion was heartily agreed to.

WARRINGTON TRADES' COUNCIL

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Trades' and Lab iur Commit was held in the Druids Hall, Sankey— street, on Tuesday evening,6lr A C Sinks presiding over a good attendance.

TRS PROPOSED, BOARD. OF )NCILISTION -RESULT OF TRU

DEPUTATION TO, THE CDAMBEEt OF COMA/RECS.

The Secretary (Mr F Strorr) reported that six delegates from the Council attended at Market Gate Chambers. last Thursday evening at the reqtoest; of the Secretary of toe Cnamber of Commerce. The deputation met the Council of the Chamber and were received exceedingly well. After some discussion the meeting felt satisfied that if a Board of Conciliation Was formed in Warrington: it would be of great advantage both to the employers and workmen. The Council of the Chamber of Commerce passed a resolution in favour of the formatioa of a con- ciliation Board, and promised to bring it before the next meeting, of the Chamber. Le, was decided that the rules observed by the Manchester Board of Conciliation should be the basis of the rules to guide the Warrington Board. The delegates were also of the opinion that four repre- eentatives from each body would be qpite sufficient. The Cnalastaor bald he accompauied the deputation, and they were received very courteously. The Chairman of the Council (Mr Parker) suggested tnet it would be advisable to have an excnange of delegates. In that I case the Chamber of Comm-trce would allow one of the Trades Council's delegates to sit on the Chamber of Corn- merce,and in return the Trades Council would allow one of the delegates from the Chamber of Commerce to sit on the Council. He (Mr Biuks) was fully in favour of the formation of the Board. It would be a means of settling petty disputes by nippiog them in the bud, and would no doubt,: create a better feeling between the masters and men. He agreed with the proposal as to the exceange of delegates, and in Conclusion asked the , COUU6II to nominate representatives for election to the Board. _ _ _

Messrs Binki, Stott, Podnuore, Piinston, Horrocts, EJ warason, Fargher, Davies, Battersby, Pacts and Pcok were n4minateri ani the election will take place at the next meeting of the Council. The question 01 the exchange of delegates was deferred until the next meeting.

The appeal read as follows: In issuing this appeal tor financial assistance for these I men, I trust you will do what you can on taeir behalf. They have received regularly the les per week accord- ing to our rules, but I think you will readily admit this SUM is small to keep a wife and family up )u, hence the necessity for some further allowance being made. The ' . following are the particulars as to how the dispute ' originated :—During May of last year, correspondeace took placo between myself and Mr Newell. the engineer. with reference to one of the men where his conditions of work had been altered. No good however resulted from this correspondence: Sims weeks after this. I received instruction from our Warrington BruCb,thit tae boiler- men and firemen at Messrs Crosfield's wished me to make application for the 48 hours' week system. I seas the above application in,and on October 11th, received a reply that tney preferred to discuss personally, any grievances of their own employees. I suggested to the men that they should sign a memorial asking the firm to favourably cprisider same. This application was signed by the whole et the men, and I sent it in on Oetober 16;n, and received a similar reply that they could not entertain a third party in cioanection with their business. On the 20th of October, I again wrote atking them to reconsider their decision, and on the 23rd of October, I received the following reply : Sir, In reply to yours of the 20th inst., the last time that we had the pleasure of discussiag matters affecting our own men, tne deputation who waited upon us agreed that in any future case they would bring what- ever they had either to complain of or suggest personally before our notice. Tnat arrangement, so far as we are concerned, has been in no way altered, and we therefore cannot depart from it on the present occasion. Yours faithfully, Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., Dr. Markel, Director." Nothing further was heard from the firm until I received this notice with the condition attached. "To Dried( water, 514. We shall not require your ser- vices after 6 cm, November 30th, 1900, J Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., G Dale, J Atherton." "If you are, however, desirous of complying with the conditions of employment as existing up to date, you may apply to the Man 'ger of the department, ,51r Dale, who will re- . engage you providing he has a vacancy to fill." On the 7tn of November' I wrote to Alderman Roberts, ' asking him if he could not see his way clear to inter- cede on behalf of the men on notice, to whica I received a private communication. After perusal of the condition of work attached to the Notice, I think yoo will see clearly that if the men had got an, "Trade Union prin- ciple about them they would not agree to any such con- dition of employment. The Warrington Trades and Labour Council have generously undertaken to issue this appeal to the Trader Uniosists or Warrington, on behalf of the men now out. Toe money subscribed will be equally divided and paid to the men now out, in addition to the allowance according to our rules (10i per week.) Tne CH6.110001 moved that copies of appeal be for.

Association of Non-County Boroughs. A quarterly meeting of the Association of non- County Boroughs of Lancashire was held on Mon -lay afternoon, at the Town flail, Warrington, for the transaction of importint business, including " the consideration of grants by the County Technical In- struction Committee,secondary edncation,returning officership at Parliamentary elections in non-county ,L borough areas, and the expediency of securing ' legislation dealing with the registration and regu- lation of clubs selling or supplying intoxicating or excisable liquors." The Mayor of Lancaster (Alder- man Preston) presided, and amongst those present was Mr Prescott, the Town Clerk of Nelson (hon. secretary), and 35 reprs-mtentatives from the Lanca- shire boroughs. The borough of Warrington was formerly connected with the association, but as it has been made into a county borough it is no longer eligible. and it was in consequence of that fact that the meeting was held in Warrington and in conse- quence of an invitation extended to the association by the Town Clerk (Mr Whittle.) The members of the association visited the new police buildings the electric lighting station at Howley, the baths, and other public institutions, and were afterwards entertained to luncheon at the Town Hall by the Town Clerk. The Mayor presided, and he was accompanied by the Depuzy Mayor (Mderman. James Fairclough).
