THE Naval Estimates The Naval Pro- gramme. continue to show a steady increase, and if the work turned out be only satisfactory the public will not mind the extra cost, and will recognise the importance of the efforts put forth to maintain our naval supremacy. Tile figure show an in- crease of expenditure for the new financial year of £2,083,600, and for this sum we are to get 3,745 more men, under various heads, and an increased activity in several branches. The Chinese Squad- ron has been augmented, some destroyers and torpedo-boats have been adaed to the Medi- terranean Fleet, and other changes have been made. The ships in hand are being pushed for ward, and the delivery of armour-plates has been improved, a fifth firm having been induced to go into the business. "The outlook in regard to the futore supply of armour is, therefore, favourable." A number of battleships, cruisers, and protected cruisers are under construction, as well as sloops, destroyers, torpedo-boats and submarine boats. Five of the last-named vessels are ordered, and experiments are to' be made with them to " assist the Admiralty in assessing their true value. The question of their employment must be studied,and ; all ries elopments in their mechanism carefully : watched in this country." The subject of mer- chant cruisers has received attention, and the vessels of the older lines have been secured under a new arrangement, and the best ships of the Orient, Royal Mail. and Pacific Companies have been reserved. "Eighteen of the largest and ! swiftest passenger steamers belonging to these companies (seven in all) will receive an annual ; subvention, and thirty steamers in addition are held at the disposal of the Admiralty without further subsidy." The Belleville boilers have been definitely condemned in an ad interim report of the investigating Committee. Ten vessels are in hand for repairs. The construction programme for the new year includes three battleships, six armoured cruisers, two third-class cruisers, ten destroyers, five torpedo boats, two sloops, and the five submarine boats, already mentioned. As to naval ordnance, there is an increased vote for re - serves of guns and ammunition, for 12in. wire guns, 9.2 in. guns, for a new 7.sirn gun, and for new Maxims. Under the head of new works there is a larger activity promised in docks, harbours, break- waters, dredging, barracks, and the like. Taken as a whole, we seem to be making almost as many extensions as if we were fighting for a better position than we have ever had before, or we had been losing ground in almost every department. Yet there are complaints that we are not going ahead with sufficient enterprise and vigour, and some of the suggested improvements are declared to be unsatisfactory. The stimulus sup- plied by the activity of other Continental Powers is, however, obvious in all these efforts, and, in our judgment, as much is being done as circumstances , warrant and the taxpayers will be able to bear. ' The navy is still our first line of defence, despite the new scheme of military organisation, which almost seems to presuppose the impotence of the Fleet, and it would be foolish to remain passive whilst our neighbours are making such rapid strides. The present programme will serve to con- vince the world that we do not intend to allow our naval supremacy to be challenged. It is ob- vious that we cannot stand still, and that our Im- perial responsibilities are increasing at such a rate as to make an extension of the navy wise and safe. Empire may be good for commerce, but it ale makes the demand upuii our defensive force greater than ever.

The Liberal Tne strength of the Unionists. Liberal Unionists in the

House of Commons is on° of all proportion to their strength in the country. We take this fact to be at the basis, to be the Un- seen foundation, of the DUKE OF Davoilsman's advice to the Liberal Unionists of Leed.s and its neighbourhood. Everybody knows that ever since the great Liberal schism the Liberal Union- ists have been a diminishing body. The Parnell Commission, the policy of coercion, the educe- tional reaction, the coalition with the Conserve- tives,and more recent events have caused a steady drain upon the numerical strength of the part,'

'

liberals have been constantly returning in nearly every constituency. Nothing but the clever elec- toral pact with the Conservatives has kept the Party in existence. To maintain this pact the fatuity of a separate organisation has to be pre- served, at great expense, and to provide the Liberal Unionist Ministers with some force to fah back upon in the event of a party disintegra- tion. But in what does a Liberal Ti nionist now differ from a Conservative? Are the various organisations aiming at the promotion of any policy different from that which is known as Ministerial? Do the unattached Liberal Unionist M.P.'s in the House of Commons prove their Liberalism in any aPecial way? Do they systematically oppose the raore reactionary parts of the Ministerial pro- gramme. It would puzzle a political investigator to discover such indications of supposed original differences of opinion in present views and acts. Wherever the Liberal Unionist be found, he can- not be distinguished except by name from the °rdinary Conservative. The Duke of DEVONSHIRE, Whilst advising that the organisation of the party shall be maintained—in the interests of the union between Great Britain and Ireland, We suppose—suggests a method of obtaining recruits that will excite some amusement in many quarters. He says that efforts should be made "to attach more permanently to our organisations those Liberal electors whose votes were recorded in our favour in the autumn of last year. Mr. ezusantaratiN cordially concurs in these views." The new Liberal Unionists, therefore, are to be sturdy supporters of what the Government has dote in the war. But some of these Imperial Liberals are Home Rulers, and they have no sym- Pathy with Liberal Unionism as such. How can they become "permanently" attached to a party Which is reactionary on Irish, domestic, and edu- eational subjects? There are also Liberal Union- -4t3 who do not approve of the present Irish policy of the Government, as witness Mr T W RUSSELL, and there are others who do not champion the C4orernment policy in South Africa, conspicuously atnongst them Mr COURTNEY. Neither of the men nlentioned is permanently attached to modern Liberal Unionism. It is, on the Duke's own show- ing, a changeful kind of party, ready to take up new subjects, but always true to the Coalition. The duty of the Liberal Unionists,in short,appears to be to keep their leaders in the Govern ment,and to support the Government because their leaders are in it. This is the whole duty of man, accord- ing to the spirit of the Duke's advice. The best Way of fulfilling this duty is, surely, to become Conservative, when there can be no mistake in the Position or its duties and responsibilities. But no; this would shock the consciences of men who have never done a Liberal thing since 1866, and injure a party which has been shedding its more honest Politicians ever since. Liberal Unionism has be- come a solemn farce. It has no policy that is not Personal and its latest " principle" is to support the Government in South Africa, where self- government is given to Colonists, whilst denied to Irishmen at home.

The Birkenhead Farmers' Club has taken a al_tr:ong line against the sparrows. Not content with ,"411g privy to the destruction of 8,000 birds last Ye.ar, it is issuing an appeal for stronger action, 7,11411 a view to the extermination of these familiar ther4ers of the feathered tribe. The case against f e BParrow is that his tastes are too clearly in ,av,our of vegetarianism. He is too fond of corn, not keen enough after insects. There have differences of opinion among naturalists as to profit and less account involved in the life of a Parrow ; but the old-fashioned farmers have stead- ily regarded these chirpy little things as enemies. at may be that the evil is one of over-population, and that the evil is not that we have sparrowa,but that we have too many. It may be that the com- Parative mildness of recent winters has lessened the mortality among sparrows.; or it may be that the fields are kept cleaner than they used to be, 'flough this seems improbable in view of the agri- rtural depression. But the idea of ,exterminat- stgit,he sparrcrws,of attacking the whole tribe,so to •freraK, seems a wanton act of barbarism, and it is o° hoped that it will not succeed. It is certain, 11?dee. d, that in the towns the sparrows will remain, ,Picking up a living in a very humble way. Until rotor-cars become general, they are in no danger °f Starving; and they have a certain use, as well • 48 brightening town life.

Whatever else may be said in favour or dis- , praise of the British Navy, it produces old men. " Humanitarian " of this month contains a Ihst of distinguished veterans enough to make the 'heart of a midshipman fill with pride. It reminds 'ape of the pity which a famous sailor expressed in lime of storm for the people on land, who had no 'apace in which to stir while the chimney-pots and tiles were flying about. There are fifty flag Officers, headed by that fine old Admiral of the Xleet with the respectable total of ninety-one Years to his credit. None of them entered the -service later than the 'thirties of last century, and ,rnost of them did so in the early 'thirties. The majority have lived under four Sovereigns. One of them, Admiral Sir George Elliot, was com- mander of the Columbine in 1839; while several were actively engaged in the bombardment of St. Jean d'Acre in 1840. Sir Erasmus Ommaneey, as midshipman, assisted in an exploit of the 3.6ritish Army in 1,827, and several others were in the Franklin Relief Expeditions fully fifty years Q. It is a plcasant story to tell—that of the "Itality and healthy extension of the ordinary in- eat in hutcan life to men who have risked so Zlieh to sustain Great Britian's superiority on the ;4mes. It is said that any lengthened time on the :4 produces rheumatism, and other ills ; but these tterans are a visible evidence of its strengthening 4%ellit•

MATTERS OF MOMENT.

A Provincial Letter.

The Duke and Duchess of Cornwall sail to-day from Portsmouth on their tour to some of the more important of the British Colonies generally, and those of Australia in particular. They have already been informed of the keen interest felt in the pro- posed visit, not only at the Antipodes, but at the various ports of call, including Port Said, Colombo, and Singapore. The Royal couple .have likewise been assured of an enthusiastic welcome at Mauri- tius, Durban, Cape Town, and St. Vincent, on their way to Canada and Newfoundland If the time- table be adhered to, their Royal Highnesses will be back at Portsmouth on the first ot November. It is no secret that the Duke, who shares with his father a love for foreign travel, has been looking forward to the voyage with some degree of eager- ness. The tour cannot fail to do good, bath from an educational point of view, so far as the Heir- Apparent is concerned, and in cementing the ties which unite the Colea,es to the Mother Country. When Royalty starts upon these expeditions the number of presents to be given away constitute no trifling detail in the Reueral arrangements. But there are other things more prized than jewelled bangles or gemmed snuff-boxes, and the Duke must appreciate the power accorded him by the King of conferring the honour of knighthood; a power which will be regarded by the Colonists as practical' evidence of the high dignity with which their Royal visitor has been invested.

This promises to b if not a record at any rate a recording month. Votes have already been recorded for County Councillors, and for many of the Parish Councils, while Urban Councils have yet, to be re- constituted, and Boards of Guardians will have to wait a few days longer for their turn On the last day of March there will be a recording of every in- habited house and every human occupant, in the shape of a " numbering of the people." The duties of citizenship have multiplied greatly within the past half-century, but inasmuch as we have been taught to regard them more as " privileges" than duties, we are not so tired of them, possibly, as might otherwise be the case. To some, however, the charm of having so often to decide the respec- tive claims of men, and of women, whom they see every day, appears here and there to have been broken,and the, enter no longer so enthusiastically into local electioneering for County Councils, Town Councils, DiQtrict, and Parish Councils as they did even a very few years since. Not that there is less interest manifested in the doings of these bodies, which, as a rule, have served the ratepayers effi- ciently and welt, but really there are now so many of them The public spirit and usefulness however, are much admired,and the chanccq are that, instead of becoming fewer in number, they will before long be more numerous.

As might be anticipated, after the revelations made in what is known as the " Allport case,' tried at Bristol, which sent a thrill of horror through the country a few days since it is being urged in various parts of the provinces that the limitations on the operations of criminous baby-farmers must be fohowed by more stringent restrictions on child-assurance. In the making of these restrictions, however, it seems ne- cessary that care should be taken not to embarra,s the honest parent or guardian. It would appear absurd to condemn the whole system of child-as- surance as intrinsically bad. Same portion of it may be made bad by cruel and dishonest persons, but the principle underlying the system is not ren- dered thus unsound, nor does it always act as a temptation. The (destruction of the system might be a blow against thrift among the poorer classes. Yet there may be round room for improvement de- spite the preventive clauses inserted in the policies of some companies. There is iittle doubt that at no remote period the Legislanire will re-examine the subject of child-assurance with the view to re- move some of its more obvious and dangerous de- fects. But what has to be considered in this con- nection is that the vast majority of honest and right-thinking parent 4 should not be deprived of such aid as may he afforded through the medium of the life assurance companies.

To-morrow (17th) is St Patrick's Pay. Local celebrations of the day have somewhat fallen off in most parts of the provinces, and on the strength of what occurred last year, when Queen Victoria visit- ed Ireland, many Irishmen have written to the papers urging the desirableness of a revival of the custom. What effect these appeals may have will be seen to-day and on Monday. The great day fall- ing this rear on Sunday, tne Irish Parliamentary party will hold their annual dinner on Tuesday. The Countess of Limerick's Shamrock League ap- pears to have been tolerably successful ; quite a number of the leading members on both sides of the House of Commons having given it their patronage. The main object is said to be the supply of the genuine article, in the shape of shamrock, through- out the British isles, "freshly picked and carefully packed," for use on St. Patrick's Day. By its sale, it is proposed to benefit that deserving charity, the Soldiers and Sailors Families' Association.

Vienna, according to the "Daily Telegraph's" correspondent, has had an experience very similar to that of Liverpool recently, in the matter of over- head electric wires. There had been a sudden and heavy fall of snow as at Liverpool, the weight breaking the overhead conductors of the electric tramway. Many horses were killed, and several passengers more or lees injured. It is thought the accident will lead to the abolition of the over- head wire. We are not quite so ready to accept the inevitable hint in this country.

--- Arrangements for the eleventh British National Convention of Christian Endeavour Societies, which is to be held at Sheffield, May 25th to 29th, are pro- ceeding apace, and there is every reason to antici- pate that the high standard of preceding Conven- tions will be upheld. The Rev John Clifford, D.U., will preach the Convention sermon at the Albert Hall on Whit-Sunday morning. The Convention Committee have arranged for a number of excur- sions for Whit-Wednesday to places such as the Dukeries, Chatsworth, Buxton, Paveril Castle, etc. Great things appeared to have been expected by t'iose who would like to see the public health authorities, or the inspectors under this rood Adulteration Acts, upon the track of that mysterious compound sold in London at fourpence a pound as tea, from the question to be put in the House of Commons on the subject. Mr. H. Hob- house submitted his question on Monday in such a way as to enable Mr. A. Chamberlain to reply that the Board of Customs had no knowledge ot sales in bond of tea "containing microbial products" dangerous to health, and that an inquiry on the subject was for the Local Government Board to consider and decide upon. It can scarcely be expec- ted that the latter will take the initiative, however, and in the meantime England, it may be supposed, will continue to be regarded as the dustbin for all rubbishy" tea" which has been refused admission to Continental countries. -- Objections to the Shops Bill introduced into the House of Commons by Sir Charles Dilke and nine other memberstand down for its second reading on the 26th mat, are being formulated and sent in from several important towns in the provinces. The gist of these objections seems to be that the general restriction proposed on the hours of labour in shops would create an entirely new precedent, and goes far beyond anything in the more recent factory legislation; also that the Bill would place the regu- lation in other hands, and thus create an artificial position prolific in difficuities and disturbance for all branches of trade. It is urged that the natural regulators of closing are the purchasers as shown .by the varigions of tee hour in large provincial towns inhaffited by different classes.

DILOWNINQ FATALITIES AT RUNCORN.

The Coroner (Mr R Dobson) has been fairly busy at Runcorn during the past week. His services were first required last Saturday, when in the pres-1 lence of a jury, enquiry was made as to the cause of 1 the death of William Garner, labourer, aged 63,wh0 resided at Perry-street, Runcorn.—From the evid- ence adduced, it appeared Garner was employed as a labourer at Wiggs works. On Thursday night he was down there, and in walking along the quay edge was supposed to have slipped upon the shiny ore and fallen into the Ship Canal. No one was about at the time, but when Garner was missed search was made and his body was discovered in the water between the ship " Emerald " and the quay wall. Artificial respira- tion was tried, but with no effect.—The jury returned a verdict of "found drowned." On Wednesday afternoon, Coroner Dobson again sat' at the Runcorn Police Court, when evidence was submitted to the effect that William Robert Collins aged 19 years, an invalid son of Mrs Winifred Cal.! lins, residing at 4, Grindrod-square, was "found drowned" in the Manchester Ship Canal, at the Old Quay Docks, the previous day. It was stated de- ceased was rather dull of intellect, and a sufferer from some form of lung disease. Re used to spend most of his time in bed, and was given a great deal of his own way. About 930 p.m. he left the house in his night-shirt, and was subsequently discovered dead in about 18 inches of water by Constable Ed- wards. Deceased was lying face down. There were no marks of violence on the body, but a slight abrasion of the shin and cheek seemed to indicate that the deceased had tripped and fallen into the dock, a distance of about 12 feet below, and being too weak to recover himself was thus suff As indicated a verdict of "found drowned" was re- turned.

_ 1 CONCERT AT VICTORIA•ROAD BOARD SCHOOLS. The boys attending the above schools gave a con- eert in aid of the Orphan and Benevolent Fund of the National Union of Teachers on Friday evening. NELSON-STREET MISSION HALL, RUNCORN, On Monday evening a lecture was delivered at the above hall by Mr Edward Boden the subject chosen being that of the prodigal son beautifully illustrated by lime-light views, which the lecturer made use of with telling effact. Appropriate hymns and solos were sung at intervals by the Nelson- street choir to give greater realism to the pictures shown upon the screen: The chair was taken. by Mr James Roughsedge, of Preston Brook, who made an. appropriate opening speech. The lecturer made the old story glow, with his eloquent and very suggestive language, and described graphically the various scenes in the prodigal's way- ward career. As the Lecturer finished speaking the Chairman called upon Mr Byfield to give a few songs upon his gramaphone, which produced roars of laughter and loud applause. At the conclusion of the programme the chairman said it had been a treat to him to listen to Mr Boden's lecture that night, and he intimidated his willingness to come again and occupy the chair. He then proposed a hearty vote of thanks to Mr Soden for his lecture, to Mr By&ld for his gramaphone entertainment, and to Mr Hoxworth for providing gratis the lime- light views. This was seconded by Mr James Strange, the presiding organist, and after loud and proloneed applause the meeting terminated. -- - TOO RE A. COLD IN ONE DAY

PLAYING WITH FIRE-ARMS LT THE THEATRE. On W ednesday afternoon a startling accident occurred in the Runcorn tneatre. Three boys named George Henry Walker, 49, Wellington-street,Josepti Clare, Wellington-street, and Charles Fox, High- street. gained admittance to the theatre, and after playing about in the pit for some time, they got some guns lying in the property room, and played at "Briton and B )er." The boys, in the course of the game, got on either side of the pit, when Clare suddenly shouted to Walker, "Hold up your hands or I'llfire." As Walker hesitated Clare pressed the trigger when there was immediately a loud repor, and Walker was shot in the neck. The noise at- tracted the attention of a youth named Abraham Kennedy, Loch-street, and he forced an entrance, and found the injured lad bleeding profusely. He at once raised an alarm, and got the lad conveyed to Dr MacLennan. There were two large wounds in the lad's neck, but he is now making satisfactory progress. The gun was not loaded with shot, but with a number of missiles which had been crammed in by the boys. They did not know it would go off. THEFT OF CLOTHING BY A WIDNES YOUTH.

RAILWAY MYSTERY AT ACTON

BRIDGE.

Body Found on the Line.

News reached Runcorn on Thursday, of a very sad railway mystery. About 6 o'clock in the morning the body of a well-dressed gentleman was found on the line at Anton Bridge. He was an rificer evidently of some steamship line run in conjunction with the railway ser- vice, for in one of his pockets was found a first-claas privilege ticket from Leeds to Manchester. The cloth- ing and general appearance indicate a seafaring man. In another pocket there was a £5 note. The body was much mutilated, and had evidently been run Over by a passing train, as one leg was entirely cut off and the other broken. How deceased got to Acton Bridge is absolutely shrouded in tnystery.and his strange and very sad end has caused considerable sensation throughout the district. Up t time of going to press his identity had not been established.

RUNCORN NOTES. [ByA CORRESPONDENT It was delightful to see several ladies out with collecting boxes, on Wednesday night, engaged in the'laudable effort of raiging the local oursinz fund. The innovetion is a commendable one and affords us all an opportunity of dropping in a copper when we can. The result, too, proves that the Runcorn people never shirk their responsibilities for the amount collected was very encouraging.

The estimates of the Runcorn Board of Guardians and the Rural District Council, were submitted at Tuesday's meeting. They show a very satisfactory eats of affairs. The Poor Rate is likely to be 8d in the .£ and the District Council rate 44. Compared with the corresponding period of last year, the Guardians raise their rate Id in the .£ and the District Council abate theirs to the extent of Id in the £ ; thus a sort of compensating balance is maintained, and the combined rate of both bodies is maintained at a uniform figure. The House Committee of the Board of Guardians, however, are face to face with the knotty pro- blem of dietary arrangements. In the master's opinion the new arrangement will add MOO to the annual cost of in-maintenance, and this figure is considered very large. One would naturally suppo-e that the new Local Government Board Dietary Order aimed at the very desirable I object of placing Boards of Guardians in a position to practically do as they liked in the matter of classification. Deserving cases are to be treated liberally ; and undeserving cases, of course, proportionally illiberally; this would imply to some extent a kind of subtraction and addition inasmuch as the comforts of the one class are increased, and the enjoyments of the other lessened. If, however, the class distinction in the Runcorn Union is not 90 marked as to preserve the cost of working at its old uniform rate, surely it cannot be expected that " our Guardians " should deny to the deserving what they really deserve, irrespective of expense. Indeed, all the ratepayers desire that their rate should go to the Poor, and if 1 Boards of Guardians studied economy in adminstra- tion rather than at the cost of the comfort of the paupers a greater public good would be accom- plished.

Helshy is indeed well ble,.,ed with public spirit, and the scramble is being n 3 keenly watched as a Arst class Rugby foot- ball game.

HALTON ROAD WESLEYAN CHAPEL

Re-opening Services.—Revival of

Runcorn Methodism.

The 20th Century scheme, is not the only scheme, which claims attention from the Methodists of Runcorn, for this week they have been engaged in enjoying the first-fruits of a scheme entirely their own. Toe scope of the Runcorn Wesleyan Circuit improvement scheme has already been fully detailed in these columns. Brunswick School has already been dealt with, and was practically the first work completed in connection with the circuit improvements ; still,the transformation of Halton-road Chapel must stand as this "first fruit" of the glorious undertaking by reason of its importance and tar-reaching effects. It was a long while ago when it was first realised that Halton-road Coapel was too large for its congregation, and though some Wesleyan, would adhere to the ideal of'* working to fill it," in these latter days the church officials have been oblig d to turn their attention to the devising, of means to improve things. The result has been that the chapel, which was built by the late Mr Thomas fiazelhurst for a suna of £B,OOO, has been literally cut in two, the upper part of the building beinc, converted into a commodious chapel witb seatings for about 700 persons, and the lower part being transformed into a schoolroom, of the most up-to-date type, with class-rooms on either side, th 9 glass partitions ot which enable the whole school to be under the eye of the Superintendent from start to finish. A feature in the carrying out of the alterations was its expedition ; on the one Sunday, ser vice was held in the old cnapel, and the following Lord's Day the flooring of vas, new one had bi•en completed and the service was conducted up- stairs. Messrs J R Frith and F J Norman, assisted by Mr Joseph Wilding (surveyor to the Urhan District Council), were practically the chief engineers ; the structural alterations being carried out by Mr Caygill the painting tad decorating by Mr Josepo Green ; and; the plumbing and gas fitting by Mr Coventry ; whitn labour has been supplied by 7.,a10as members of the congregation in spare time; thus enabling the whole work to be carried out slightly under the estimated cost of £BOO.

On Munday evening a circnit tea meeting Wag held in the school, which was filled to overflowing. The tea was dispensed by the following ladies :—Mesdames Arrowsmirh, Morrison, Foster, J R Frith, J M Frith, F J Norman, E Taylor. R Morris, Coventry, &Angler, Lloyd, Goulden, Cooke, Johnson, Beswick, and Miss Taylor. Subsequently a meeting was held In the chapel, under the presidency of Mr J R Frith. There were present : Revs W Arrowsmith, A Wells (superintendent of the Runcorn Circuit), and H Morrison, Messrs F J Norman, S Taylor, A R Norman, T Simister, S Pope, E Taylor, R El Posnett, J Pd Frith, L Rosenberg, jun., J Looker. J W Bazley, J Brown, Johnson,Graham,George Bellamy and others.

_ The Rev H Morrison opened the meeting with prayer, and called upon Mn J R Frith to take the chair, Mr. J R Frith:on taking the chair said a lady asked him that afternoon if he felt proud, but he replied 'No I feel thankful." (Hear, hear.) He was profoundly thankfcl to God for all His mercies to himself, and to Halton•road. Seme 25 years previously it was realised that Halton-road chapel was too large for the congrega- tion that was likely to attend it, and the idea of cutting it in two was first conceived. 83rne 15 years later he made a proposal to the trustees at their annual meeting that .he work should b 3 done. The matter was thought over, and it was realised it could be done without getting into debt. He had great aversion to getting into debt. (Hear, hear.) He was therefore very i thankful to God that the beautiful sanctuary, I in which they were assembled, had been opened free of debt. (Hear, hear and applause.) He was also thank I ful to God for several other reasons ; the work had been i completed :without a single accident—(hear, bear); and because the alteration had been proved a great atlcCeii, and was likely to be the means of great blessedness. (Hear, hear.) It had been said to him when he first mooted the question of cutting the chapel in two " 0, you will spoil the place." Had they spoilt it ?—(" No, Ne "I —Let them look around them and look below. (Hear, hear.) Na, it was not spoilt, and he was glad to think they had now such a beautiful church in which to worship God ; and, to see, they had such a teautiful schoo'room. There were two or three things they had been obliged to consider. First there was the nursery of the church. (Hear, hear.) They wanted a good schoolr iorei—a luxury they did not possess at Mill 11:ow —and the idea was therefore, how Co get a good schoolroom, for they could not afford to build, hence the inception of the scheme which on being so admirably carried out gave them a good schoolroom below and a good chapel above. (Applause:) Indeed, he felt he could say without boasting there was not a schoolroom in Cheshire comparable with their school. (Loud ap- plause.) Having referred to the discomfort experienced in the old chapel owing to its vastness, Mr Frith con- traeted it with the cheerfulness and compactness of the present structure.Ttey now kne v each other—(laughter) —were connected with each other, rejoiced with each other, and could sympathise with each other in ti ee of need. (Applause.) He believed that the future of the schools would be prosperous. They had been so in diffi- cult times, and when circumstances were adverse,there- fore he felt they would be more so now. (Appiause.) The Church was already prospering, and souls were be- ing brought to know their God. If having a good school , and a good church had been the height of his ambition, his ambition would be poor indeed. It would be miser- ably poor. His arnbition,however, was that the Church should prosper ; that souls should be saved ; that their young people should be brought to God, and that they I should see the chapel filled with worshippers—not cold, lifeless creatures like they had been for a long time past. but worshippers filled with life and vitality. He be- lieved that the time bad coma when the Lord would revive them again as in the days gone by, and when it I came he could say as Simeon of old, " 0 Lord, lay me down in peace." Might God grant it. (Loud applause). I Mr F J No-man, who was cordially received, in an ex- ' ceedingly enlivening speech, recounted the difficulties which bffet them at the inception of the scheme, but ' which thanks to the loyalty and devotion to the cause, completely vanished when the work was taken in hand. He wished to pay Mr Wilding a naeed if praise. He had given them gratuitously magnificent service in connection with that scheme. (Applause.) Mr Wilding would give them a certificate that that place was now safe and really very artistic. He (Mr Normaa) did not think any of them would say it was perfect—there was nothing perfect on earth—but he wanted those kind friends who did not think they had got all they wanted to accept the place as it was, and do their best to make the church and school a great success. Referring to the circuit scheme, and the alterations elsewhere be intimated Brunswick was practically finished, Halton-road was pra3tically complete, and Weston was finished. They had now Preston Brook Chapel to deal with, but they did not propose to tackle that work until the spring. He was not going to say anything about the financial posi- tion of these undertakings. Their worthy treasurers, Mr 8 Taylor and Mr Frith, would no doubt be able to surprise them when matters were wound up. He thought they would fled that these gentlemen bad something up their sleeve, but he did not for one moment want them to cease giving on that account. (Laughter). He wanted to know from their Halton-road friends what they were going to do P Be was there to assert that they could not blame their tools, and to maintain that if the Runcorn circuit, as they believed it had, had given them all they wanted they had a perfect right to expect them to do toinething for the magnificent Methodist cause. (Hear, hear.) Were they proud of Their Methodism ? Did they realize what a blessing it had been to them individually ? Did they believe it was the Metho list revival touching the work- ing men and the middle classes of this country that changed the whole aspect of affairs. Look at Runcoru. Let them carry their minds back to the men who trut-..18 Runcorn, men whose names they honoured to-day, and they would find a very large percentage were Wesleyan Methodists. or Methodists of some denomination or other. Then with regard to the Empire generally. It was nothing but the result and effect of the Methodist revival among the working meta and the middle class, s that saved them from revolution. That was not the hair-brained statement of a Methodist ; any good his• torten would tell th,,tri that. If they went further, to their colonies, they found it was for the most part their , Methodist pluck and enterprise that had gained them their magnificent Empire. All over the world they hid the most important positions occupied by men of their Own religious belief. Were they satisfied with the pre- sent position ? The " Standard " once said that Methodism had done a magnificent work in the past, but her day was done, and the sooner she sank bank into Oblivion or returned to the church the better it would be for all concerned. There were a good many people who believed with that writer in the " Standard." but their late great and good Queen Victoria had no such notion as that. What stru k him most Particularly about their late Queen was that no good organizstison in this country wanted for a word of sympathy from her. (Applause.) No institutiun that

was trying to do good,although the general public might not know it, lacked the kindly sympathy and apprecia- tion of the Queen. She wrote letters of sympathy and appreciation to the head of the Salvation Army, and the Methodist people, and so long as they could be quite certain that they had the sympathy and support of toe Sovereign and the highest officers of the land tney might go on fearlessly with their work and ignore other opinions. (Hear, hear, and applause.) No man need have any misgiving an commending Wesleyan Methodism to anybody, he did not care who it was, the highest, the lowest, the richest or the poorest, the most learned or the illiterate. (Hear, hear.) He waisted them to go tor ward with the determination that they would play their part in that locality. He did not want them to proselytize. (Applause.) Deere were a good many Church people, Presbyterians. Primitive Methodists, and Mttnodista of other denominations in the district, and no charged them before God, as they came into con- tact wet) these people, not to attempt to make Wesleyan Aletbodists of them. Tnere was no more wicked thing than to attempt to unsettle the religious belief of another man. Their esteemed superintendent the Rev A Wells, wrote a kindly letter to the Rev Canon Wood, pointing out that in the recent Free Church Mission a numoer of Cnurch people had received good, and had passed into the inquiry room, and concluding by recommending them to tue vicar, and asking him to kindly look after them and try to gee. them to their own church. (Loud :applause.) That was the spirit in which all religious work wanted to be carried on. While they were caretul not to proselytize others they ahould not allow people to proselytize them. He wanted them to ground themselves in the faith, doctrine, and polity of Methodism. If they would do that he had not the slightest fear of them being taken away by anybody. The same remark applied to the teaching of their children in their own schools. Tee present turn in educational matters would throw a far greater responsibility upon toe Sunday schools than they had had to bear in the past. Methodism raised toile were ut of the gutter, and made men of them, and so far as they suc- ceeded in doing that they were doing a magnificent work, and they were doing Ged's will in the most em- phatic manner they could possibly do it. (Loud ap- ' plause.) Mr R H Posnett said nothing had given greater plea- sure to the people in that church than to see Mr Frith in the chair. He rejoiced to see Mr and Mrs Arrow- smith with them again, and wished them every ems- perky under God's blessing in the new circumstances in which they found themselves. (Applause.) Toe Rev W Arrowsmith, who was also cordially re- ceived, said it was a great joy to come and preach at the Sunday services. Tney could not know how ereat a joy it was, It was a far greater joy to his wife and nimself to come to that meeting than it was for the friends to ask them. (Applause.) Tbeir life might be a happy life—it was a happy life—out there was a good deal of cruelty, in his opinioa, in Nonconformist ministers' lives. They felt the influence of friendship and love, and they liked to be with people whom they knew and loved. Eveey now and then Conference stepped in and ordered them about, and au re was no saying "no." They were ordered to move on, and really the Methodist ministers lives, and the lives of their wives and families would be unbearable, were it not for the extreme kindness of thought of the old friends asking them to come back occasionally. (Hear, hear and applause.) He never indulged much in going out of his circuit to preach ; but, if he possibly could he never refused to visit an old circuit. (Hear, hear ) He had many experiences since leaving the Runcorn cir- cuit. From Runcorn he went to St. Helens, across the river, not a very beautiful neighbourhood—(laughter)— but the Methodist people were grand people. (Hear, hear.) The•ecclesiastical atmosphere of the town and neighbourhood was dense, and to his mind not very in- spiring or invigorating. Sacerdotalism was decidedly in the foreground, and Nonconformity very much in the background. There were three good Nonconformist churches there—the Wesleyan, Presbyterian, and Con- gregational Churches—but they were in a helpless ml nority. His hearers could know but little of the indigl nities to which they had to subunit. In his c'rcuit, at that time, they had a very large Sunday School, contain- ing some 400 or 500 children, but, for those children there was no educational provision of a s-cular nature save in the Church of England and Roman Catholic Schools, and they had the humiliation of standing by and seeing their Methodist children driven in- to the Roman Catholic Schools. The Methodist parents noticed something about their children and asked what it was, and they replied "0 you must net touch that, that's a sacred thing given to me by the sister." He knew of several Methedist families—or at least one Methodist family—awakened during the night by tbeir children (making remarkable noises. When the parents went to the bedroom, the children were smiting their breasts and calling upon Mary. Fancy Methodist chil- dren submitting to such indignities (shame). Well, he happened to have a little advantage in that district, which he did not possess in Runcorn, he was superin- tendent, and had the right to express his opinions and emphasise them. (Hear, hear.) Be said in the name of the Methodist Church, such matters should last no longer, and although they had no day schools of their own they would have one. (Hear, hear.)An appeal Was issued to the people with the I •sult that they magnifi- cently responded, and he had the joy of seeing erected a magnificent day school, fully equipped, and their Methodist children preserved from all the dangers of sacerdotalism. (Loud applause.). From St. Helens he went to Huddersfield, and from thence to his present circuit, (Stockton), but he was not going to dwell upon those places. He was optimistic in all his thought and in all his experiences. Who could have stood at that desk the previous day (Sunday) or down in the schoolroom below without feeling that Runcorn Method- ism was renewing its youth by pulsating with life and vitality; also, that as certain as they were living men, the glory of Methoeia al in days to come, in connection with that sanctuary and allithe sancturarie's throughout the circuit—the glory of Methodism in the future was going to be greater than it had ever been In the past. (Loud applause). There never was a time in the country's history, in which was more needle i the simple, earnest teaching of Methodism, than in these days. It was by the expounding of the Gospel in its simplicity and purity that the resolutions which brought about so much good came to pass. Finally the rev speaker contended they. bad something more to do than teach people to accept Jesus Christ. They had to teach them that sin was hatefnl in the sight of God, and that re- pentance was necessary for the salvation of the soul. (Loud applause). The Rev A Wells first conk the opportunity of thank- ing his many kind friends for the hearty welcome they had extended him on his return after a complete week of rest. (Hear, hear.) Then he congratulated the wo kers on:the admirable consummation of their project. He was charmed at the manifestations visible of return- ing power in the Wesleyan circuit of Runcorn. One of the poets of old said something about : Lot The winter Is over and past and gone. The voice of the people Is heard In the land, And the time of the abasing of birds Is come. (Hear hear and Applause). Old grant it may he true with them—(hear, hear,)—and it would be true just in proportion as each one realised their individual responsi- bility and determined to apply the latent power that God had given them. It would be a diserace to Run- , corn Methodism, if it is ever again heard, that they could not get a weekly prayer meeting at Halton-road. (Hear, hear.) As placed at the head of the circuit he was jealous not merely for its financial well-being, and its numerical prosperity ; but more jealous still for the honour not of the section of the church they belonged to, though no one held more strictly by that than he did ; but he was jealous for the honour of Christ's Church on earth, and for the salvation of the number of souls around them. Mr. Wells then moved a vote of thanks to the Rev W Arrowsmith and Mrs Arrowsmith, the Chairman, the choir, and to all who had assisted in any way in the good work. R.v Ii Morrison seconded this comprehensive vote of thanks, which was carried unanimously. The Rev Arrowsmith, the chairman and Mr J Brown (on behalf of the choir) responded. The singing of the Doxology concluded a splendid meeting.

TEIR SALE OF INTOXIC&NTS TO CHILDREN.

Tne Lancashire and Cnestiire Band of Hope and Tem. perauce Union took the liberty of calling toe attention of the B wird to the efforts now being made to get the Law or the country in respect to the sale of drink to chit. dren amended. At present children of any age may buy beer and spirits for consumption off the premises; children of 13 and upwards may buy and drink beer on the prem., ises,aud children of 16 and up wards may buy and drink either beer or spirits on the premises. Toe recent Royal Commission on Licensing in both the majority and minority reports, rec,mmended " That no child under 16 should be served with intoxicating liquors for con- sumption either on or off the premises." This proposal had been embodied in a Bill, backed up by representa- tive numbers of all parties, which Mr W S Crombie M. P., had introduced to the Hou,e of Commons during the present Parliamentary Session. The second reading was down for Wednesday, March 20th, 1901. Therefore, on behalf of the 1500 Societies in all parts of Lancashire and Chtsbire, with over 250,000 members whom they had the honour to represent, tuey respectfully appealed to the Board to memorialise the House of Commons and Uovernm nt in favour of the proposal. A copy of the dill was enclosed.

The letter WAS ordered to lie on the table. PURE BEER.

In a circular letter Sir Cuthbert Quitter, writing on be- half of the promoters of the Pure Beer Bill, said that with refereece to the resolution passed by the Board urging him to persevere with the but, he was glad to be in a position to inform them that the motion for the second reading of "A Bill to amend the law relating to the manufacture and sale of beer" had the first place on the Orders of the House of Commons for Wednesday 27th March. He hoped the letter would go before the Board at their next meeting with an expression of his hope that they would both officially and individually do all in their power to ensure the presence and support of their local members of Parliament on that occasion. It was, he said, important that members of the House of Commons should be unmistakably assured as to the wishes of the majority or their constituents in the matter, as they would no doubt, have considerable pressure put upon them to vote against the Bill by those who were pecuniarily interested in the manufacture and use of glucose and other substitutes. The Board deci led to repeat the procedure taken when the Bill was first brought to their notice, and re- quested the Clerk to write to the local members of Parliament requesting them to support the Bill.

THE 20TH CENTURY.

Do you know that it has often been said that great events happen at the end and at the beginning of a Century ? No doubt we shall have the usual prophets , forecasting the probabilities of all sorts of startling occurrences to mark the commencement of the 20:h Century, but there is no one bold enough to predict that there will be less of pain and suffering, less of human misery and disease in the World. The lot of human kind is to suffer, and the greatest benefactor is he sotto does something to mitigate pain and affliction. "Never prophesy till you know "is a wise saying, and we adept this maxim when we say that Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters is destined to occupy a greatly extended sphere of usefulness in the new Century. It has been proved by thousands of sufferers to be the one safe and certain remedy for all Nervous Disorders, Low Spirits, Depres MOP. Liver Complaints, Indigestion, and Genera Weakness.

BEFORE the MAGISTRATES

A WRETCHED WARR/N(ITM./ HOME.-" NOT F/T FOR A FIG TO Live IN."

At the Warrington Police Court on Saturday, Mary Ellen Harrison and George Harrison, who reside in Aikin-street, were charged with neglecting their four children in a manner likely to cause them unnecessary suffering and injury to healtb.—Ntr. A. Browne prosecu- ted on behalf the N.S.P.C.C., and briefly stated the facts of the case.—Mr Bomber, school attendance officer, gave evidence, and stated that he went with Inspector Hackett to the house. Ti ere was no food to be seen. Witness provided clogs for the children on the suggestion of Mr. Hackett, to enable them to Ro to school.—The rooms were in a filthy conditien.—Police-Sergeant Smith said he visited the house on the 15th January, and found it in a filthy state, The smell in the bedroom was unbearable." Hand other visitors had to 10-070 the I room and go downstairs for the female defendant to open the windows.—lnspector Hackett said on his first ' visit to the house he had little fault to find with the chil- dren but the house was Wry dirty. The condition of the woman was also very objectionable. On a second visit to the house the woman smelled strongly of liquor. The body of the baby was in a shocking condition, the other children poorly clothed, and in a filthy state.— Tie found the house on his second visit in a worse condi- tion than before. The male defendant told him he gave all his money to his wife. He was not so much to blame as the wife. He was really afraid of her.—At this stage the charge against the male defendant was withdrawn. —Dr Bowden said he examined the house on Tuesday last and found the children well nourished. Caroline had sores on her head, her hair had been cut short, and some attempt bad been made to improve matters. The clothing of the children was scanty and dirty. The house W&A net fit for human habitation. The kitchen was in an especially filthy condition. The enrroundings were such as to endanger the health of the children.— The Cliairrnan.—A decent pig would not live in such surroundings as those which have been described.— D fendant was committed to goal for three months.

PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

MOST of us live in a continual state of expecta- tion of somebody doing something. NONE of us want to hurt anybody else's feel- ings ; but it sometimes seems necessary to our own peace of mind. JowEs "Why don't you join our shooting party?" Smith: "Well, rm a poor shot, and I'm afraid you will make game of me." DOUBLY BROTHERS.—Robert Browning was once introduced to the Chinese Ambassador, the intro- ducer, who acted as interpreter, observing that they were both poets. In the conversation Brown. ing asked how much poetry his excellency had written. "Four volumes," was the answer. "And what style of poetry does your excellency cultivate?" "Chiefly the enigmatical," replied the other. "We felt doubly brothers after that," was Bkrowning's subsequent comment. Savisn By ::::TlL.—" Go!" she exclaimed, and never speak to me again!" He passed out into the unsympathetic night, but paused when he reached the sidewalk, and drew something from one of his inside pockets. As he did so the beauti- ful girl who was watching him from the vestbule uttered a shrill scream and ran towards him. "Albert," she cried, "what would you do ? Pray do not kill yourself ! Throw your revolver away, and let us forget our quarrel." "It isn't a revolver," lie replied, as be caught her in his arms. "It'd a spanner I borrowed from you that day my wheel broke down. I have been wearing it next to my heart ever since." They then returned to the parlour and took up the thread of love's yonng dream where it had been broken.

"My dear," whispered the young man, "as we are so soon to be married, we should take a practical view of life and profit by the mistakes of others. For instance, there is the subject of a regular allowance every week for spending money, you know." "Oh, I've thought of that," she replied, sweetly. " Have you!" "Yes, indeed. Hundreds and hundreds of times; and lately I haven't thought of much else." "You know the amount of my income, and that I want it to go as far as pcootible towards our happiness?" "Of course. NORTHERN UNION CUP COMPETITION: *-

Second Round. Warrington v. Hechmondwike.

Warrington safely negotiated round two on Saturday at the expense of the Yorkshire Second Division leader-, Heckmondwike. The latter club brought a strong team and had some hopes of success, but the fifteen which Warrington put into the field was altogether too smart for them. Fine weather favoured the fixture and this in conjunction with Warrington's excellent performance the previous week, caused the crowd to roll up in large numbers. There were about 8,000 spectators round the enclosure and the gate receipts totalled £ll5. Mr Itsnton, Hunslet, was the referee, and the teams lined out as follows : Warrington : Hallam, full back ; Fish, Isherwood, Dickenson and Harris, threequarters ; Bate and Duck- worth, 11%If backs ; Mara:lan, Eden, blereweather, Scholtze, Edmondson, Fe'l, Swift and Cunningham, for- wards.

Heckmondwike : Stead, back; Bland, Sharpe, Dawson and Arnold, three-quarter backs ; Stirk and Lloyd, half-backs ; Gath, Phillips, Sykes, Gough, Foukill, Settle, Pickering,and Garnett, forwards. Warrington won the toss and commenced operations with the wind and sun in their favour. The visitors started and found a footing in the home half, whence they were 110011 dislodged by Fish who punted atrongly to Sharpe, the latter being grassed by Boardman. War- rington at once attacked, Fish finding his course blocked after gcod passing by Warrington. Bate and Duck- worth were very smart, and giving to Dickenson the latter ran strongly before parting to Harris, who was stopped at the corner flag. The movement was repeated the wing man again failing by inches to penetrate the visitors' defence. Duckworth marked in front of goal, and Fish safely sent the ball through the sticks, but the point was negatived through the home forwards getting off-side. Strong pressure on the rail- way goal was maintained, a long punt from Hallam being t. uched down by Stead in the nick of time. Bate had his kick charged down and the visitors' front rank rushed the game into the home half, Isherwood saving finely. The Yorkshiremen staved long enough to register a dead ball, and then Warrington beat them beck. Fine passing by Duckworth, Isherwood, and Dickenson completely :beat the defence, and the last- named scored after a strong run. The position was too far out for Fish, who made a good attempt at goal. Heckrnondwike retaliated with neat passing, Bland punting into the home half. Duckworth and Isherwood smartly exchanged on the left, and the latter parting to Fish at centre saw the wing man race through the visitors' ranks and score behind the posts, afterwards adding a goal. Another minute and the ball passing through the bands of Bate, Duckworth, Dickenson, and Isherwood enabled Fish to force his way over again, at the corner. No goal resulted. Warrington were easily superior to the interval. Half time :—Warrington, 1 goal, 3 tries (11 points) ; Efeckmondwike, Swift recommenced, and alter a few minutes even play one of the visiting forwards retired with a damaged shoulder. Warrington seemed inclined to take matters a bit easy, and Dawson nearly dropped a goal for Heck- mood wike. The same player kicked across the field for Bland, and Dickenson saved on the home line when a score seemed probable. Fish led the way to the other end and made a wonderful effort to dodge his way through the whole Eleckmondwike team. Sharp, did good work for the latter and forced Hallam to defend. Warrington passed again, Harris made another strong effort which just failed tit the corner. Quickly the oval was whisked across to Fish's wing, and the speedy youth crossed at the corner, cantering unopposed behind the posts, and kicking another goal. The visitors had more of the play for a time, bit the Warrington forwards dribbled back again very effectively, Fell making for the line and being tackled by Stead. Sharpe got the ball and threw to Arnold, but Harris stepped in and taking the ball at his toes went over the line at the corner with a smart try. Fish failed at val. Heckmondwike made another burst to the Warrington end, and were at last rewarded, Settle kicking a good goal from a penalty. Final : War- rington, 2 goals, 5 tries (19 points) ; Heckmondwike, 1 goal (2 points).

Runcorn v. Leigh.

Runcorn came as triumphantly through round two as they had through round one. Their second victims were Leigh, who visited Runcorn on Saturday buoyed with hopes of victory and encouraged by the shouts of nearly a thousand supporters, who had made the journey in two special trains. The weather was delightfully bright and pleasant, but the ground had not recovered the re- cent persistent rainfall and was very heavy. Leigh were short of their crack forward,McMasters and Runcorn bad to find substitutes for 13ayrer and Reece. Prompt to time the teams lined up as follows : Runcorn : Houghton, back; Jones, Butterworth, Richardson, and Warder, three-quarter backs; Faulkner and Jolley, ball-backs; Farmer, Walker, Lighdoot, J Langley, Tomlinson, Woods. Hughes and S Langley. The visitors lost the toss and kicked off with a brilli- ant sun shining in their faces, and Faulkner made a good reply. Runcorn at once got into their stride and treated the 4,000 spectators to some smart back play. the ball being passed with accuracrand despatch. At the CClleiUSiOn of one bout of excharges J Faulkner made a desperate attempt to cross the line, but be was held up a few inches outside. From the ensuing scrum- mage the ball was quickly transferred to D Jones, who grounded over the coveted mark, but the ball was called back. Next moment consolation came Brilliant pass- ing amongst the backs, landed the ball to Faulkner who 'Msde no mistake and scored a very clever try. Jolley failed to add the goal p,iats. At half-time Runcorn led by eleven points to nil. After turning round Leigh played up with surprising vigour and for a considerable time operations were confined to Runcorn half. Repeatedly the Leigh forwards gained possession in the scrummages but the Leigh plan of kick and rush did not come off with the veterans facing them playing in cool and con- fident style. Clare. Runcorn v. Swinton. The Blues Annihilated.

On Wednesday evening some 6,000 spectators flocked to witness the contest between Rut c ira an 8 winson, at the Irwtil-lane enc'osure, hundreds coming from Swinton, Oldham, Warringem, Widuee, and the locality in high expectation of witnessing a capital fight. Still he deserves credit for manfully doing his best under the circum- stances. Hardly had the game been reopened when Jolley was given a chance with a penalty. His effort was neer, however. A New Fire Engine for Lymm. The Lymm District Council have long felt that the 013 manual fire engine was insufficient for the wants of the neighbourhood, and this has now been replaced by a splendid new steam lire engine by Messrs Rose and Sons, of Manchester, a trial of which took place on Saturday. Besides Mr W Arthur Dewburst (chairman of the District Coun- cil), most of the other members and officials of the Council were present. Representatives were present from Messrs Rose and Co., and there was a large gathering of the general public at the trials near the mill. An ample supply of water was obtainable from the dam, and a grand force of water was soon thrown twice the height of Mr Henshall's house, which is one of the tallest in Lvmm. Double lets were tried, and the mill received a good washing, besides which jAts were Set at work in the hollow, and sent columns of water over the highest of the tall trees. MISSION SERVICES.—The first services of the mission at the Trinity Wesleyan Chapel, conducted by Mr John Weston, of Aroside, commenced in earnest on Sundsy, there being a much better, congregation in the morning then usual, and a large attendance at the evening ser- vice. WARRINGTON

NEW 156Libt - BUILDINGS. Progress of the Works.

A number of Warrington pressmen paid a visit of inspection to the new constabulary buildings for the purpose of reporting the progress made in the erection of the structure. Previous visits of pressmen have been associated with official functions. The first visit in 1899 was in connection with the laying of the foundation stone by the late Alderman Wright, and at a more eecent date several pressmen screwed up enough courage to follow the Mayor to the dizzy heights of the topmost chimney cap, where a pot was laid with as much form and ceremony as the unusual surroundings permitted. There was nettling so remarkable in connection with the visit of which something has now to be written. The pressmen were escorted through the premises by Mr Hallard, the genial clerk of works, who wended his way through a labyrinth of unfinished corridors and rooms amid a be- wildering maze of poles and scaffolding with an ease that was thegenvy of the uninitiated. The pressmen followed him as best they could, stepping gingerly from joist to joist, and negotiating gaping cavities with • courage that seemed to commend itself to the busy plas- terers, joiners and labourers who were met with at every turn. The purpose to which each room was to be put was described by Mr Renard, who was a storehouse of information on everything connected with this hand- some pile of buildings which is rapidly approaching com- pletion. The progress made since the laying of the foun- dation stone, sixteen months ago, has been rapid and continuous, and it may now be safely anticipated that the opening ceremony will take place in the autumn of this year. The exterior of the new buildings is practically com- plete, and the architectural features disclosed are such as to warrant the opinion that the structure will be an ornament to the town. The terra-cotta dressings are of a cinnamon tint and blend beautifully with the brick- work, and as the sun's rays glint upon the surface, the details of the ornamentation are thrown out in relief, and produce a pleasing effect. There will be three en- trances to the new buildines ; two of them from Arpley- street and one from Wilson Patten-3treet. The public and police will make use of the Arpley-street entrances whilst the magistrates will have an entrance to themselves from Wilson Patten-street. It will there- fore be seen that the complaint facetiously referred to by Mr John Croefield that at the Irlain-street police court it was difficult to distinguish magistrate.; from criminals on account of there being only one entrance, will be re- moved when the new buildings are in use. Entering for the nonce by the magistrates' entrance, the visitor would fired himself in a wide and well-lighted corridor, from which access is gained to a number of rooms on either side. By turning to the left a room built for the use of the Watch Committee is reached. Here, all the business relating to the police force will be trans- acted. It is plainly but nicely fitted up and is light and airy. Near to, is a room for the use of the police' surgeon and • little farther away, but quite separate from the main building, is a mortuary and a room for conducting post mortem examinations. Retracing our steps to the magistrates entrance,on the right is:situ eted the Chief Constable's room, with a strong room for the storage of valuables and important documents, whilst near to is the office for police clerks, and rooms for storing uniforms. &c. The detective department is furnished with a commodious room which has windows looking out on to Wilson-Patten-street and Arpley- street. The public entrance is tha centre one, and the police court is reached by a stone staircase. The court is 52 feet by 40 feet and will provide ample accommodation. At the present time the plasterers are giving it a second coat. It will have a panel ceiling with ornamental moulding work. The bench will he handsomely fitted up, provided with a canopy which will be embellished by the Royal Coat of Arms. Retiring rooms for the magistrates and the clerk are close at hand. The magis- trates, clerk will sit immediately in front of the Bench, and excellent accommodation has been provided for the solicitors and reporters. The dock will be in the centre of the court and there will also he two witness boxes. The public will be accommodated in a gale, y at the back. In close proximity, are rooms sat apart for male and female witnesses, consultation room for solicitors and clients, solicitors' room with robing room attached, and a reporters' retiring room. Within easy distance is the Corener's Court. This is a replica of the Police Court on a smaller scale, the dimensions being 441 t by 21f I. Adjoining it is a retiring room for the jury and another for the Coroner. Retracing our steps to the police entrance which is the one nearest to Museum-street, one finds a telephone room and rooms for sergeants and inspectors close at hand. A criminal on being arrested would find himself escorted through this entrance, and immediately taken into the charge room right opposite. Aojoining it are two rooms to be used for searching prisoners, one for males and the other for females. It is but a few steps from here to the cells. Fourteen have been provided for men and seven for women. They are light, airy and well-ventilated, and if one were disposed to contrast them with many of the dwellings of the poor in Warrington, he might safely say they were superior in every way. They are built lof enamelled brick, provided with patent unbreakable lights, and doors lined with sheet iron. Lavatories and airing courts are contiguous to the cells, and the whole arrangements are such that even such a prisen reformer as John Howard could ecarcely have dreamed of. All one gazes at the stone staircase which leads to the judgment hall, one shrinks from anticipating the great number of characters who in the near future will ascend and descend them. There will be excellent accommodation for the police. A dormitory is being erected for fourteen single con- stables, and there will be • reading room, recreation room, and mess room fitted with all modern require- ments. There will els° be provided on the site occupied by the old armoury, a covered-le parade ground, 97ft by 501 t, and accommodation for married constables and caretakers. A unique system of ventilation will be adopted. Fresh air will be drawn by an electric fan from ventilators On the roof and carried through air tight tubes and ,distri- buted throughout the building. By this means a uniform temperature will be maintained. The buildings will be heated on the low pressure system with radiators fixed in convenient positions, and lighted throughout by I electricity. Mr. C W Davenport is the builder, and the architect is Mr Burns Dick of Newcastle-on-Tyne.

SANKEY BRIDGES WESLEYAN CHOIR.

Councillor Brough on Church Music.

At the invitation of Councillor and Mrs. Brough, the members of the Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Choir met in the schoolroom on Monday evening for the annual social gathering. Between 40 and 50 res- ponded to the invitation. A capital knife and fork tea was served and thoroughly enjoyed. After the removal of the cloth, a programme of games, agree- ably interspersed with vocal and instrumental items, was proceeded with. At the outset, Mr Miles proposed a vote of thanks to Councillor and Mrs. Brough for their hospitality, lie referred to the many acts of kindness which Mr. Brough had extended to the choir, awl stated that they could hest. reciprocate it • by loyally supporting him as their conductor in any work be took up.—Miss Morrell, on behalf of the lady members, seconded the vote of thanks, and paid a graceful tribute to Mr. Brough's services as conductor of the choir, and of his kinelness to the members of it.—The vote was unanimously accorded.

Councillor haouon, in responding, said he had never replied to a yore of thanks with more grAtimele in his heart than to the present one. Tue proposition had aa additional interest lent to it from the fact that a lady had seconded it. As a choir tlsey had to supply music in connection with their place of worship, and music in the church was a subjerit of vital importance to evvry chvgich ot ro-day. Music touches and stirs emotions that can be awakened i their fulness n no other way. Music is rno of the handmaids of religion, and the church that eschews music. Or fails to make a right use of it, it, is sure to be a cold and stunted member of the bony of Christ. Therefore it follows that the music of the sanctuary should, for the most part, be such as all can j,in in, and all should join in it to the best of their pdwera. The spec.acle presented in some churches, where the congregation silently sits and i stens to a performance renders d by t he choir, composed at least occasionally, of s number i of men and womrn who have only a professional interest n their work, is by no means edifying. Worship should never be done by proxy, and though the heart. may accompany, with its silent melody some min mat rendering, sweetly rendered by more skilled voices, yet Ira contended no re- ligious service shou'd he herd cc which the thief musical items were not within the reach of all. A.iain, there Should be variety and beauty of musical torrn in our service of praise, with the ,pread ot general and especially of musical ethleatiOn We .have Come t.) appreciate the ordeis and yarn-tics of sacred music as rover beim° We enjoy a series of hymns and tunes if chants and anthems, nowadays, such as the world has never bad before. The great musician—Sir Arthur Sullivan— who has just passed away, was only one of many who have enriched the treasury of sacred inusic with price- , less contributions, now within the reach of every Con- gregation to use as a means of expressing ita common adoration and praise. It is a great benefit also that the old prejudice that so long hindered the introduction of variety into our musical worship, has passed away, and that there are few forms of sacred music that are net welcomed ty most congregatiuns. There had, indeed, : been a wide-spread cultivation of Ml.lbied faculty during the last generation, and it was bearing fruit in a richer, ampler, and more refined service of praise, (Hear, hear.) Should he give them a sketch of the ideal congregation in relation to worship ? It was a very

simple one. The service would be one that would com- bine some of the simplest with some of the more deve- loped forms of music. The people would all have books containing music as well as words. They would all see to it that they hal studied the art of singing, at least,so far as to be able to take their right place in the balance of voices, knowing how to sing at least,all hymns,turtes, and all the chants, and most of the anthems itai common use. They would be ashamtd to be silent when the time came to join in the praise of God. There would b 3 a choir, but it would not take the place of the congregation, but skilfully ; lead it,and give it direction and confidence to sing more I worthily, and it would give an occasional selection of its own, which would be beyond the power of untrained voices. The choir would be composed of men and women, spiritual in character as well as skilful in voice, and full of the power of the Holy Ghost. With such a choir and such a congregation, there would be no limit to the spiritual blessinss that might come through the door of song. People would come to such a setvice,and find their minds quickened, their better nature roused. The wayward would feel the call of their higher self,and the devout would feel that this was indeed none other than the House of God, and the very gate of Heaven. (Applause.) During the evening a number of interesting competi- tions were held, some of the prizes having been given by Messrs Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Lmited, consisting of I samples of their specialities. Songs were efficiently sung by Mr D Dunn and Mns William Taylor. Mr Walter Enough, jun., was deservedly encored for a violin sole. The accompaniments were played by Mr E Wilkes, A.L.C.M. and Mr T Brough,

A YOUTH'S SHOCKING DEATH Fatality at Messrs. Greening's Works. A youth named Joseph Ashbrook, 17 years or age,who resided at 75, Laira-street, Warrington met with • a shocking death whilst following his employment at the works of Messrs. Greening and Co., Scotland-road, on Wednesday last week. It appears that he was placing some paper " packing "on the roller of a wire weaving machine, when his head was caught between the sleigh and the roller, and cru.hed. Death was instantaneous. Mr S Brighouse conducted the inquest at the Borough Police Court on Fiidav afternoon. Mr W H Draper appeared on behalf of the firm' and the deceased's relatives were represented by Mr T S Steel. Major Roe. H.lll.'s Inspector of Factories. was present on behalf of the Government and Mr A J Willett watched the proceed- ings on behalf of the Ocean Accident Insurance Company. Thomas G. Hewitt, a boy, aged 14, said he was a needle winder at the works and was assisting Ashbrook on Wednesday morning. Deceased knelt down for the purpose of winding two sheets of paper " packing " OR the roller of the wire weaving machine, and told witness to give the machine one " shoot." Witness did so and decelsed raised his head and caught it between the sleigh and the brass roller. Witness saw him fall and sent for the foreman, but when assistance arrived deceased was dead. .

Mr Sceel : The paper would not catch in the roller until you gave the machine one shoot ? WitneFs N Sir.

Further examined witness stated that one shoot brought the sleigh to within three inches of the roller. The dec-ased was kneeling down with big head below the machine roller.

In order to facilitate matters a sketch was handed to the jury. Mr Draper said his contention was that there was no necessity for the deceased to get underneath the • machine.

Witness further stated that he had never before seen anyone place thc paper on the roller in the manner he had described. It was cuAtomary for them to kneel in front of the roller and place the paper on with both hands.

The jury returned a verdict of' Accidental death." On behalf of the directors of the Company, Mr Draper expressed regret at the occurrence an extended great sympathy o tha relatives in their trouble.

LATEST NEWS.

CHINA.

Friction between British and Russians.

===== 0002601_19010315_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
British Ordered to Use Armed Force

if Necessary.

PEKIN. Tuesday,

Friction has arism ' at Tientsin between the British and Russians over a piece of land which is claimed by the Railway Cimpany as having Iran in their possession for some years, and which the Russians declare to be part of their new concession, and therefore Russian property. Mr Kinder, manager of the railway, who had com- menced constru-ting a skiing on the land in question, was stopped bp the Rusdan authorities Be appealed to Colonel MacDonald, who referrred the matter to Pekin.to General Barrow, chief of the Staff, who replied, "Carry on the siding, by armed' force if necessary." Guards were therefore placed along the line, and the work was continued. The Russian General Wogark protested, and said that the Brirish would not have acted as they had if Russia had had the same number of troops as Great Britain. He added that such matters should be left to dipl nnacy. Colonel Mac- Donald asked General Barrow for further instruc- tions, and received, "Continue the siding." Ganeral Wogack is very indignant, and has appealed to M. de Giers.
