THE WAR.

Another Runcorn Man Joins the Yeomanry.

The Imperial Yeomanry has received another recruit from Runcorn in the person of Henry Hill, son of Captain Hill, of Surrey-street. Hill belongArl to the Runcorn detachment of the 2nd V.B CR. He was accepted for the Yeomanry after the usual examination in shooting and riding. A Frodsham Volunteer's Letter. letter received from Private Fred E7ans and dated Janu try 26'h, 1901, says —"I: expect you have seen in the papers about our camp b ing struck by lightning. We thought our tent wos struck in the first instance,but bappily, the lightning just missal us, and struck the N.C. Ols tent and killed one of the corporals of the third section, and severely wounded two lance-corporals, one belonging to our section and one to the first section. In addition to this it slightly hurt three or four non-com- missioned officets. We buried the poor corporal yester- day. A storm here sometimes makes terrible havoc of property, the rain falling in a perfect deluge, fl toding everywhere, while the thunder and lightning are appal- ling. Everything seems completely upset here. through the lamented death of the Queen, and yesterday the artillery fired 82 minute guns, one for every year of the Queen's life.. We have been told_to-day that General . .

iitebener's last order was that Volunteers wnuld start

for home after the Bth March. and as we were al,out the •first in the country we should be among thp first going bonne. We should arrive in old Eland tor Easter. Ladysmith. Described by a Warrington Lady.

Writing to her fatuer,the Rev. F Slater, vicar of Over, (and late vicar of St. James' Warrington), from the Crown Hotel. Ladysmith, undcr date February 2ad, Mrs Hordarn, wife of the Rev. AV C Hordern, chaplain to the forces, states : lam all alone in Ladysmith, an I do not think I like it. A. left this place this morning at live for Pretoria, and they tell me here it may take two, three, or four days to get there. This is a very nice little hotel, a sort of bungalow, very camfortable, but absolutely empty, which is somewhat lonely for poor me now. I must tell you of our journey from Durban here. It was a most wearying performance. We left at 6 p.m. and arrived here at 6 a.m. The carriage was full until we got to Pietermaritzburg at ten, and there everyone got out, and we had the carriage to ourselves for the rest of the night. We slept until it was just beginning to think about getting light. We got to the battlefield of the awful Colenso and Pieters Hill, and we passed within a dozen yards of young Roberta's grave. It looked most beautiful, and there is now a white cross over it. The relief column fought their way in along the railway line, so we are passing along the very route. It was rather dreadful to see a little odd crave here and there, where men had fallen, the grav2l lasing marked out by little crosses made of wood or tin, or in fact anything that would make a cross. Here and there were also big graves railed in,where I suppose men have been buried in numbers. We crossed the Tugela, and the bridge the Boers blew up. Of course, it has since been repaired. We also passed that awful intombi were over 700 were buried. We could distinctly see the graves and crosses, and A. has shown me the exact spot where his tent was pitched just before we got into Ladysmith. We also passed the river where the Biers tried to damage the besieged and flood the town. The sand bags are still there as the Boers left them. The officials are most par- ticular about letting people on the railway ; and such a railway ! It is a single line the whole way from Dur- ban, and this jolting Up and down over the most awful precipices is cruel. No one is allowed out of here with- out a warrant. A. has applied to the Commander at Harrismith for permission to bring me in, and we are awaiting his answer in fear and trembling. If he refuses I cannot think what is to be done, for the expense of living will be awful. I shall probably have to go to Estcourt, as Ladysmith is too unhealthy. It is simply reeking with enteric, the heat is beyond endurance, and the water quite out of the question for drinking purposes. It is positively alive. A pint bottle of the commonest claret costs 2s 61, and that is the cheapest wine there is. Lemonade coats Is a bottle. Ouraide the window as aleroup of jabbering coolies and Kaffirs selling peaches and pine apples. Peaches are about twopence per dozen, and I bought some pine apples for twopence each, rut I believe I was cheated as they are a peuny each in Durban. This house was occupied hy Denise' lan Hamilton during the seige and has three shell holes in It. One shell came right through the roof, the draw- ing-room wall, and entering the back room it killed the Colonel's servant who was laying breakfast. They have not repaired the town much yet. There is a piece of shell inserted in the pavement in memory of a well- known doctor who was killed on the spot. It is the very piece that killed him, and his name and date of death are engraved thereon. He had both legs blown Off. Writing on February fith, the same lady states got back to-day after a most exciting railway journey. The Boers are all around Johannesburg and Pretoria,and the line is guarded by soldiers, who stop the train every now and then when they think there is danger. The train creeps along slowly, but I have got • him back safely, thank goodness. Also another blessing, My pass for Harrismith arrived last night, I am extremely lucky, as everyone is being refused. Two poor ladies have been waiting a fortnight for permission, and they have just got their answer, which is " no." I must now stop as it is late, and we leave for Harrismith early in the morning. We are both flourishing. I might add that Harrismith is in the war area."

THRILLING TALE OF THE SEA.

-- Board of Trade Inquiry. • A thrilling tale of the sea was told at the Board of Trade inquiry, held in Ginslow. last week, into the cir- cumstances attending the loss of the British steamship Strathleven, a vessel built tw Messrs Blackwood and Gordon, Port Glasgow' in 1876, and owned by the Strathleven Steamship Company, of which Mr Henry Abram, brother-in-law to Mr James Lea, Runcorn, was the manager. Sheriff Scott Moncrieff presided and the nautical assessors were Captains Parfitt and Wood, and Mr J H Hallett engineer. Captain James Innes said his vessel left Pensacola, in Florida, en 20th December with a cargo of 1990 tons of phosphate rock and 749 tons of cotton, her destination being Barcelona. All went well until eight &el >ck on the nighc of the 6th of January when a strong gale blew from the south-west. An hour later the sea rose to such an extent, that he found it implssible to keep the steamer in its course. He accordingly allowed her to run before the wind until eleven o'clock,large quantities of water being shipped in the interval. At 11 o'clock he hove her to, the decks at this time being continually flooded. At two o'clock on the morning of the 7 h Jan uary the a Ind changed to the North-west, from whence it was blowing with hurricane force, and accompanied by a heatsy cenfused sea. A heavy sea was shipped on the port side about half-past two with the result that the port lifeboat was smashed, the front of the saloon broken, and the port side of the engine room casing carried away flash with the deck. This casing along with the skylight fell into the engine room, where it carried before it gratings, ladders and other fixtures, at the same time blocking the bilge Innen and the sluice sounding pipe: Consequently the water swamped the engine room and in about 20 minutes the fires were extinguished. The open spaces were repeatedly covered with sails, nailed to the deck, but on each occasion the work was carried away. The ship fell into the trough of the sea when the engines stopped and then oil was used to prevent the sea 'breaking. The tunnel door was closed and an attempt wade to start the donkey-pump while at the same time the captain opened No 2 sluice, and allowed the water to tun into the second hold, so as to reduce the quantity in the engine room, on account of the continuous seas, however, it was impossible to work the pump, so that after half an hour, No 2 sluice was closed, the crew meanwhile using buckets to throw the water out by the ventilarors. At seven o'clock the starboard life-boat fell to the deck and was smashed. Two hours later a barque was :sighted which turned out te be the Ardgowan. Flags of distress; wereisnisted and signals exchanged and finally a bnat wss put off and came alongside. With con- eiderahle difficulty the crew were got off in twoljourneys, a consultation with the officers leading to the unanimous opinion that it Was unsafe to remain on boarn, The Ardgowan cruised about in the vicinity all night, but in the morning there was no sign of the " Stratb- ' !even " which must have foundered. The crew were eventually landed at the Azores the vessel having fOnndered about 100 miles south-west of that point.

... The Court found that the vessel was well equipped and wag not overladen, and that ehe encuntered excep- tionally heavy seas, which broke, over the veagel, damaged the engine room casing, and broke the skylight. through which the water found its way to the engin. room and ultimately pot out the fires ; that every effort was made to repair tne damage done by the s?as to pre- vent the water Rhipped from going below ; and that the olip was not prematurely abandoned. No fault wag attributed to anyone for the loqa of the ship. The Ceurt also referred with satisfaction to the assistance rendered to the crew of the Strathleven by the Ardzowan, and o the great irrs,nal elk incurred in effecting the rescue C the crew. •

Madame Davies appears to have the choir under complete c-Introl, with the result that the combination and precision of at,alk. are points of excellence worthy of the greatest praise. It would be a most difficult matter to select any of the choruses for special mention, but the competitive piece. "Spanish Gipsy " fairly brought down the house and earned a vociferous encore. All the solos were equally wall rendered, but the item which gained ti e most enthusiastic applause was the scene " Misereri "from the opera of "Ii Trovatore "in which the F:ol3iSt was Miss Mary Thomas, the pure deep contralto of Miss Mary Jenkins, who as:isted, adding great charm to the selection. The audience were enraptured and loudly demanded a repetition of the treat,the artistes gracefully acceding to the request. Several other items were encored including, "The Old Plaid Shawl," by Miss Mary Jenkins and the song and chorus 0 The Swallow "by Miss Annie Bell. Special praise must be accorded Miss Back- sheen Wood,whose contributions on the violin were vociferously encored. Of equal merit was the performance of Miss Marie Novello Williams on the pianoforte and so appreciative was the audi- ence that they asked for a further exhibition of the clever pianist's Dowers a demand which she readily acceded to. Altogether the concert wis a great Success, and there is no doubt the hospital fund will greatly benefit. PIIESENTATIoN TO MR. THOMAS SWIFT,

• EARLESTOWN. On Saturday evening a number of the employes of the Sankey Sugar Company, with several friend, assembled at the Griffin Hotel, Earlestown, for the purpose of marking their appreciation and esteem of Mr Thomas Swift, who for 21 years had been employed by the Sankey Sugar Company, and had now been suclessful in obtaining the appointment of clerk to the Gas and Water Department, of the Newton Urban District Council. Mr Edward Pair- dough, cashier to the firm presided, and after observing that all present would be aware of the object of the gathering, proposed the toast of "The King." This was suitably honoured, the company singing the National Anthem. The business of the evenicg centred round the presentation, which was made by Mr E Fairelough, who reviewed the number of years he bad been connected with Mr Swift, which had extended over 21 years, and he spoke in flattering terms of that connection. On behalf of the employes of the Sankey Sugar Com- pany, be formally handed to Mr Swift, a handsome marble timepiece. An inscription will be attached to the clock, as follows:—" Presented to Mr Thomas Swift by the employes of the Sankey S 'gar Com- pany on the occasion of his appointment as clerk to the Gas and Water Department." The eulogistic remarks made by Mr Fairclough were supplemented by Messrs J Walker, J Haselden, 13 Dolan, A Wrigley and T Knoney, all offiAalsl of the firm. Mr T Swift thanked all those who had honoured him by subscribing, and those who had spoken for their kindly expressions. He dwelt upon the consider- able amount of help be had had from the late and present manager and the facilities which had been afforded him in attending classes for self-Improve- ment. The other important toast was " The Sankey Sugar Company," which was proposed by the chairman and responded to by Mr T Haselden. Other toasts were "Our visiting friends," and "Host and Hostess." An excellent programme of songs &c was rendered during the evening as follows, song "The Drum Major," Mr W Beatty ; song," Simple young man," Mr E Forrester; song, "In Friendship's Name," Mr .1 Gibbons; song, "Down in the deep," Mr J Ingham ; song (humor- ous), Mr J Huddlesten ; song, "In the pale Moon- light," Mr T Beatty; song," Queen of the Earth," Mr J Gibbons: song," Dewn by the Deep Blue Sea," Mr J Appleton ; song, "The Skipper," Mr Higbam ; recitation, "He nal to stand along in line," Mr T Kenney.—A vote of thanks to the chair- man terminated a most enjoyable evening. It may be added that prior to these proceedings, Mr Swift was presented with a £5 note by Mr Thompson on behalf of the firm.

----- On s Wednesday, the beautiful afternoon and the pros- pect of a great game at Runcorn tempted me to wheel my way to the banks of the Irwell. where Swinton were due to do battle for the premier position in the League. Everyone receenised that the match WAS a crucial one for the " Lions," as defeat would make Oldham a point better off on the matches played. Moreover; Swinton having been knocked out of the West Lancashire and Northern Union Cup Com- petitions were very eager to secure the League trophy. All these considerations conspired to make the fixture a most attractive one, and in consequence the Runelrn Club had the best gate of the season, Warrineton sup- plying a goodly number of enthusi‘stq. and Oldham also contributing a few, including " Jack " Hurst.

Runcorn bad their best team out, Swinton lamenting the absence of R. Valentine and Lewis, and the odds therefore were clearly in favour of the 4. Linnets." The game commenced at 5 o'clock, and before five minutes pas: the hour Runcorn were a try ahead as the result of Ilev's irrepressibleness. Though Swinton had the ' wind they were badly over-played by the Runcornians, and Jolley scored again before the interval. There was some fine play on both sides, and Swinton exerted them- selves to the utmost. They got a try in the second half, hut Runcorn got a couple through Jones and Richardson, the latter also dropping a tine goal. At the end there- fore Swinton were soundly beaten by 14 points to 3 points. The winners were far the better team and played a game which few, if any, clubi could have sur- vived. Jolley was in marvellous form, and in addition to always having a hlnd in the scoring, he did more defensive play than Houzbton. The other men were all good, the forwards being much the better set. Dal Davies was the hest man for the losers, and played with determination, pluck, and skill which deserved a better reward. For Swinton to get the championship Oldham will have to go down at Leigh, which is a contingency not very probable, whatever the Mather-lawrs think. The Blues seem to have lost all Chances of glory for this season, by reason of their sad deterioration.

"ANCIENT MONUMENTS AND FOSSILS."

Mr Reginald Potts wrote calling attention to the Ancient Monuments Protection Act, 1900, whiclii em- powers County Councils to ineserve either by purchase or by some arrangement with the owner any monument any structure. erectian, or monument of historic or architectural interest or any remains thereof, Be was instructed by the County Council to bring the provisions thereof under the notice of the District Council, and to inquire whether there were any such monuments situate withiu th4r district, and if so to be furnished with particulars of same. The County Council would he prepared to consider any representations that might be made to them with a view to putting the act into. operation.. _ _ _

The Chairman : Have we anything ? The Clerk: You have old castles. I think they would c'tine under it. Mr. Linaker : Yen have several "old fossils " in the district. (Laughter.) Mr Farrington: There are one or two old stocks in the district. There is one at Budworth. The Chairman : Yes, some people would be bettor in the stocks. (Loud laughter.) The next business was then proceeded with. _ _ WARRINGTON TO NORTHWICH LIGHT RAILWAY.

Messrs: Brown, Brown and Murphy, solicitors, South- port, forwarded draft order with reference to the pro- posed Warrington and Northwich Light Railway, which had been amended in accordance with the requirements of the Light Railway Cmamissioners. The Clerk submitted several points of difference between the amended draft and the original one, and it was decided to defer the matter to the consideration of the Parliamentary Committee.

For the convenience of the racing public desirous of journeying to Haydock Park on the occasion of this steeplechase meeting,the Great Central Railway Company announce in our advertising columns that they will run fast excursion trains to Haydock Park (Race Course Station)which adjoins the course on each day. Accident to a Porter, Ralph Norman, an outside porter employed at Bank Quay station,was proceeding along Golborne- street on Tuesday afternoon wi,h a band-cart taining a commercial traveller's luggage, whenlhe slipped and fell on the ground. The contents of the hand-cart, which were somewhat heavy, fell upon him, and he received such severe injuries thm he had to he immediately removed to the Infirmary. Burning Accident at Wilderspool. A shocking burning accident olcurred at Wil- , derspool on Tuesday. A girl named Dolly Mosey, 16 years of age, and living witn her parents at 4, Greenbank-street. Wilnerspool-road, woe lighting the fire yes•erday morning, when her flannelette skirt became ignited. She received shocking burns on fur arms, back and thighs before the flames could be extinguished. She was taken *o the Infir- mary, where she lies in a critical condition.

United Patriots National Benefit Society

(Orford Branch.)

The annual tea party in connection with this branch took place in the Club-room, John-street, when about 60 member and friends sat down to tea. The after-meeting, was presided over by Mr H Densham, who said be should be glad to see more co-operation amongst working men, not only in this but in other ways that would be to the working man's advantage. He had looked through the annual report, and had found that financially the society was in a very sound state, and he had no hesitation in recommending its advantages. He hoped many young men would take the oppottunity of joining such a splendid society. He would be pleased to become one of its honorary members. Bro. J Brailey (branch chairman), on behalf of the mem- bers, thanked the chairman and his good lady for being present. No doubt the society would be pleased to add his name to the list of honorary members Afterwards Bro. W Joslin received at the hands of the chairman a gold pendant for inter- est taken by him in the society.

WARRINGTON.

--- Personal, The Warrington Chamber ot Commerce has bean represented at the annual meeting of the Associated Chambers, held in London this week, by Mr. C. E. Parker (president), and Mr. Arthur Bennen (secre- tary):

A Memorial Window in the Training College Chapel. On February 2ul, 1900, Mrs. Baker, lady superin- eiident of the Training College from 1849 to 1884, passed away; and those who had known her and her work felt that some lasting memorial of her ought to find a place in the colle2,e she had loved so well. A fund was immediately opened, and con- tributions have been received from humerous friends and " old students.'" These have been ex- pended in a memorial window, which his been placed on the north side of the chancel. It consists of two lights, each depicting two scenes connected 'with our Lord's birth and early 111 e. The colour- ing is very beautiful, and the details are very clear and in perfect harmony with those of the other windows in the chapel, all of which are the work of Messrs. Burlison and Grylls. A service for the dedicatiol of the window was held on Saturday, and was attended by Mr Baker, accompanied by several members of his family, and old friends. e Amongst those present were:—The Very Iti3v. th n 3 i Dean of Manchester, the Rev Cano and Mrs Pen(ryßhenv,thNeiorltleeyv Canon and nadMrasnd Btsvenson, W th Princi- palthe Rev II A and Mrs uester, Dr .. .1 Miss Watkins Mr Charles and the Misses White, Mr and sf„ Amos, Mr and Mrs T D Buckton, Mr and Mrs Dew- hurst, Mr 0 Lofthouse, Mr and Mrs P C Price, Airs Rose, Mrs Sharp. Mrs Smethurst. Mr and Mrs J C Thompson, Mr and Mrs E Wools, and Mr and Mrs W B Woods. The former members of the college staff, who had worked with Mrs Baker, Mrs Nick- rietcn(hlterir were also present, and Mrs Shepherd (Miss esent, as were all the present staff, numr.rous former students representative of the years 1819 to 1884, and as many of the present students as cou'd be accommodated.—The Principal said the dedicatory prayers, after which an address was given by the Dean of Manchester from the text "A mother in Israel " (Judgas V. 7).—After the hymn "Christ shall gather in Ms own" ha ct I)Fffln sung, the Rev Canon Penrhyn pre sounced this B diction.

NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the next GENERAL QUARTER SESSIONS of the Peace for the COUNTY OF CeIESTER will be held at the COURT HOUSE. KNUTSFORD, in and for the said Couaty on TUESDAY, the 9th day of April next, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when the Grand Jury will be empan- nelled. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Hundreds of Bucklow, Macclesfield, and Northwich, forming the Knutsford District, and the trials of all prisoners com- mitted from such Hundreds, will take place at such Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Knuts- ford District must be in attendance at the Court House, Kmitsford, at the opening of the Court on TUESDAY, the 9th day of April next, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. THE COURT will be Adjourned to the Castle of Chester, on WEDNESDAY, the 10th day of April next, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. The hearing of all Appeals arisMe within the Hundreds of Brewton, Eddie- bury, Nantwich, and Wirral, forming the Chester Dis- trict, and the trials of all prisoners committed from the last-mentioned Hundreds will take place at such Adjourned Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for Ithe Chester District must be in attendance at the Castle of Chester, At the opening of the Court on WEDNESDAY, the 10th day of April next, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. REGINALD POTTS, Clerk of the Peace for the County ef Chester. Chester, 12th March, 1901.

ELECTION OF GUARDIAN% TO THE ELECTORS OF ST. AUSTIN'S No. 2 WARD: LADIES AND GENTLEMEst, My term of office having nearly expired, I once more offer my services as a Candidate for the position of Guardian for this Ward. I have been a member of the Board of Guardians fo- the I last three years. and during the time named, I have attended nearly every Board and Committee meeting. which has been held. As a member of the Relief Committee I have always strongly advocated and supported in giving adequate relief to the cases which have come before us, to treat the deserving Poor in such a manner as would help them to tide over their temporary difficulties and prevent their homes being broken ur, and if you again honour me with your confidence. I can assure you that no efforts of mine will be wanting to fulfil the duties of the positipn. I am, Irtmrs faithfully. THOMAS 11. ASHCROFT. 135, Sankey-street, Warrington. March Ist, 1901.

OHMYS CIRCUS. Clowns Sue the Proprietor for Wages. An interesting case, remitted from the High Court, came before Judge Coventry, at Preston County-court, on Tuesday. The plaintiffs, Lloyd Brothers, musical clowns, of Barnsley, self d the defendant, Mr Joe Ohmy Smith, proprietor of " Ohiny's Circus," at present per- forming at Preston, to rec)ver £23, one week's salary for services rendered. Mr Wright stated that plaintiffs wrre engaged by defendant for the week ending January 28tb,at a salary of £23, plaintiffs, to give a couple of turns nightly, and abide by the rules of the circus. The whole case turned upon that unfortunate event, the death of th 3 Queen, which occurred on Tuesdayasnuary 22. Plain- tiffi had given their performance to everybody's satis- faction on the Monday. Mr Ohmy heard of the Queen's death shortly after seven o'clock, but did not promptly close the circus. The house was rapidly filling, and at 7.3 o—the time for the porformance to begin—the place was packed. After plaintiffs had given their turn Mr Obrny informed the audience that under the circum- stances it was only right that the performance should terminate. Tickets were given at the doors for the matinee on the foil iwing Saturday. Plaintiffs afterwards gave their performance nightly, and also at the matinee. On the Friday a notice was posted up in the circus to the effect that a ' half-night's pay would be deducted from each artist's salary owning to the abandonment of Tuesday night's performance. When one of the plaintiffs went for their salary he was informed :by Mrs Ohrny that £1 13s 4d would becleducted. Be said he;would take the £2l 6s Bd, but would only sign for that:amount, whereupon he was told that unless he signed for the whole amount he would get nothing, The sole issue, counsel said, was whether defendant had a right to make the deduction. The rules laid down were that "If there was no play, there should be no pay," but he contended there was a play on the night in question. 11!s Honour gave a verdict for plaintiffs for the amount claimed, with costs.

ELECTION OF GIIMIDIANS.—TO TFIE ELECTORS OF ST, AlisTIN'S No. 2 WARD. LADIES AND GENTLEMEN. Having been asked to become a candidate for this W ,rd and received the promise of surmort from many fr -nds and leading residents, I have very great pleasure in ing my services, Knowing the needs and condition of the Ward as I do, and having a large business connection in it which rAquires regular and personal attentlon,l am in a position to look niter your interests to the very best advantage. If elected, my efforts on the Board would be directed to "economy. conjoined with efflcleney.• while the Brabazon &theme, and the division of the in mptes for the special treatment of the watk and deservlnolwould have my sympathetic support. Being a trades Unionist, and affiliated the Trade and Labour Council, I should support the Insertion of the fair wages clause in all contracts entered Into with the Board. Either myself 'or friends will have the pleasure of wait- ing upon you prior to the election. Trusting to have your hearty Co-operation, and to be favoured with your votes. I remain, Yours Sincerely, RICHARD ATKISSON, 16, White-street, Warringion.
